## [01] Saturday Landslide Creates New Barrier Lake Near Disaster Zone; At Least 85 Americans Among Those Unaccounted For In Floods; At Least 900 Workers Missing Across Nepali Hydropower Projects; GOP Frets Over Talarico's Huge Money Lead In Texas Senate Race; Sir Anthony Hopkins Launches New Music Career At Age 88; 780-Plus Dead, 3,000 Missing After Floods Along China-Nepal Border

[14:00:03]
FREDRICKA WHITFIELD, CNN ANCHOR: Hello again, everyone. Thank you so much for joining me. I'm Fredricka Whitfield.
And we're tracking the latest breaking developments on the desperate search and rescue efforts underway on the Nepali Chinese border following those catastrophic floods.
Today, the death toll has risen to more than 780 people. Some 3,000 still missing, including almost a thousand workers at various hydropower projects in Nepal.
Emergency workers are now pumping air and dropping food into one tunnel, but have yet to establish communication with anyone inside.
And today, displaced survivors are facing new concerns after another landslide formed another barrier lake that officials fear could lead to more flooding.
Help continues to arrive in the flood zone. A shipment from China has landed in Kathmandu, and the U.S. is sending more than $3.5 million of aid.
CNN's Will Ripley joins us now from Nepal. Will, what's the latest on the rescue efforts there?
WILL RIPLEY, CNN SENIOR INGL CORRESPONDENT: Fred, the rescue efforts are ongoing, although on the Chinese side, the report has been that they have not found any new survivors over the last day or so, which is incredibly discouraging for all of the people whose loved ones were at that border crossing between China and Tibet, the scene of some of the most horrific images from this flood, when that massive black wave of water destroyed a building that appeared to be anywhere between four and seven stories and also washed away a row of busses.
But there were a handful of survivors from that area, including an Italian couple, Stefano and Valentina, who I spoke with earlier. They said, as they just barely escaped with their lives perhaps by a matter of just a few seconds, they looked down and saw that wave and what it did, the destructive power of it. And they said, it's difficult to imagine that anybody caught in the middle of that could have survived.
Here's more of my conversation with them earlier today.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
VALENTINA PICCININI, ITALIAN FLOOD SURVIVOR: Everybody turned right. Only me turned left. So that's the point. I don't know why, but my mind, I can -- I don't know, I cannot explain. I cannot see anything, anybody around me. I cannot hear my name because they are only -- everybody is saying my name. But I was not able to hear them.
So I'm just following Hernan and he showed me the only way I could follow to survive -- the only way. Because if I'm going straight on for sure, I would not be here.
RIPLEY: Did you know it was water that was coming when you saw the big black --
STEFANO LUTUMOLO, ITALIAN FLOOD SURVIVOR: No, we couldn't know. Like as you see in the images at some point (ph), it was the beginning. And we just saw something crashing into busses 30 meters in front of us. It was very big, like it was crazy. The sound, it was unbelievable.
PICCININI: I felt that everybody of them, they were alive. I don't know why, but my heart can tell me, ok, don't worry. They are all alive.
So first start, think about you. Start to run. Think on -- and survive. And then somebody, somehow, universe, God, Shiva, whoever will find a way to bring you back to them or bring them to you.
RIPLEY: And you said the healing power of the Nepali people has kept you here and helped you to heal from this experience.
LUTUMOLO: We were together. They were not different between Italian and Nepali. We were just human beings, survivor, and we just try. Also the night time celebrating life.
And they lost everything. They lost everything -- people, houses. They have nothing. They had nothing left.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
RIPLEY: They know that they are among the very lucky ones. We actually spent the whole day yesterday in Bidur, which is one of the hardest hit areas.
And I was chatting with people there who have lost, in some cases, almost every member of their family a woman who lost 17 family members, a man who lost eight family members.
And they said there are also entire families that were killed and have not been reported in the list of the missing because there simply is nobody left to add them to that official list.
Also, bodies presumably buried under deep layers of quicksand along the flood route.
So the sense I got, and I'm still trying to process it, Fred, is that we need to mentally prepare ourselves for the fact that the number of missing currently at 3,000 could actually be far, far higher than that.
WHITFIELD: Wow. So Will, we showed video yesterday of people rescuing with their bare hands a six-year-old girl who was buried underneath a lot of rubble. You could hear her, you know, crying. What's the latest on her?
RIPLEY: Her name is Manisha. And as you said, she's six years old. And it was -- it is one of the real, real joyous stories of this horrific flood because the armed police force released the video of her rescue 60 hours after the disaster struck.
[14:04:51]
RIPLEY: Her parents, who are also alive, we have learned today, they had given up hope of finding their daughter, Manisha. They were just hoping that rescuers could find her body so the family could have closure.
So when they were able to dig through the rubble of that building and pull her out alive, and they've now airlifted her to a hospital here in Kathmandu. And we've learned that just within the last several hours, she's been reunited with her mom and dad, and the family is now back together. They're all being treated for relatively minor injuries, considering the circumstances.
It is a true, in the words of many people on the ground here in Nepal, a miracle. And people here really do need news of a miracle amid so much uncertainty, so much heartbreak, and so much tragedy yet to be really be discovered, because there are even areas that rescuers have yet to reach, Fred.
WHITFIELD: Yes. Miraculous and very hopeful.
All right. Will Ripley, thank you so much. We'll check back with you. Appreciate it.
U.S. Officials say there are at least 85 Americans still unaccounted for in that flooding. Several of those missing Americans were taking part in group hiking trips in Nepal. They were right near the Nepali- Chinese border when the flash flood hit.
And among those missing Americans is Kesava Rao Tadipatri. The Arizona man sent his family a group photo of himself and his fellow hikers, you'll see him. He is located in the upper left corner of this picture that you just saw. And his family remains hopeful about his whereabouts.
I'm joined now by his daughter, Ramya.
Ramya, my heart goes out to you and so many families who are looking for, you know, their loved ones. Tell me about where you think your father may have been. You had
gotten that photograph, which was taken shortly before his hike. And tell me what you know.
RAMYA TADIPATRI, FATHER AMONG THE MISSING IN NEPAL: Thank you so much. And thank you for -- thank you for having me on.
I, so what we know so far we know that, my dad was on a recreational hike with this group and did -- and seeing various holy sites. So he spent some time doing that in Nepal. And then was traveling through the Gyirong Port. He was marked on his way to, into Tibet, and he was marked as departed from that port on at 8:30 local time.
And the last member of his group was marked as departed at 8:35 local time. That picture that you showed was -- we had received at 8:36.
And the tremors, the first tremors, we understand happened at 8:37. Then at 8:42, was we received a message from my dad and he said I will have some issues near the Trishuli River, which was that river that was flooded.
And my mom asked what issues and then it was a communication blackout that was -- that was the last we heard. And then two minutes later was that video, that showed the flood hitting that port, that very same port.
WHITFIELD: Do you, have you been able to ascertain kind of the distance, you know, between the location where he sent his message of saying, you know, I'll have some issues and, and what we've seen as it pertains to that video.
TADIPATRI: So we, we don't know where exactly he was when he sent that message. And, you know, we know the time -- the timestamp of when he sent the photo, we don't know if that was necessarily when the photo was taken or if he, you know moved on somewhere and then sent the photo from there. So it's a lot of unknowns, a lot of possibilities.
WHITFIELD: And where are you able to get some of this information or, you know, the updates, even the information about, you know, marked as having left, you know, certain areas.
is it the -- the group that organized the trip? Is there still some communication that's able, that you're able to have with people, whether they're, you know, on site or in that vicinity or stateside?
TADIPATRI: So my mom has been kind of the point person, you know, getting all the -- all the different information we've been in contact with the U.S. Embassy. And she's on a WhatsApp group with ten families that were -- that were in the -- the other families of the -- the other members of the group.
[14:09:49]
TADIPATRI: So they've been just actively reaching out and trying to get information. So there's an official list of, from the port that, of all the -- all the people and the times that they departed from it. WHITFIELD: So Ramya, tell me about your dad, you know, and this trip,
how he was looking forward to it, why this was important to him, what he was looking forward to and with these people that he was with.
TADIPATRI: Yes. So my dad, you know, he's an adventurer. When he sets his mind to something, he'll do it. He's 73. He ran his second marathon earlier this year.
And we took him -- we took him -- my husband and I took him hiking in Sedona in May of this year.
And, you know, he had his bucket list. This was something that he really wanted to do. He is also a spiritual guru and guide for the Hindu community.
So he teaches religious scripture and Hindu tradition, scripture and philosophy. So, very frequent classes to virtual classes to people all over the country.
And, so he's been a guide to, you know, helping people get through, get through difficult times. And been married to my mom for 30 plus years and she's been inspired to kind of take on, to learn and carry on that same mission and be also -- be a spiritual guru and guide to the community.
And, you know, we're -- we're all handling this, you know, the best that we can, you know, obviously shocked and worried, but, and my mom probably handling it better than, better than the rest of us.
So she's recalling, you know, the -- their marriage -- their marriage vows. And so my dad was explaining everything, all the, the Sanskrit chants and everything to her.
And she would talk about so there was one ritual of placing her foot on the stone and it's to the bride, you know, by stepping on the stone, you become the rock that will weather any storm.
And yes, she sent out a message to the community saying, saying I'm fine, you know and, she wants to be an inspiration to all of the -- all of her students and the entire community and staying strong and using our scriptures and our teachings to get through it, understanding, you know, God gives us all, all different kinds of challenges. And we work through it.
And I have, a lot to learn. I -- whether or not I can be from my dad or still holding hope, but a lot to learn from my mom for sure.
WHITFIELD: Well, we are very hopeful for your dad, Kesava Rao Tadipatri and hopeful for you and your whole family. And indeed, he's as you describe him, he's a rock and he's been a rock for your family and such a source of inspiration.
Ramya Tadipatri, we're wishing all the best for you and your family and are very prayerful for you
TADIPATRI: Thank you so much. And I want to say, you know, we're very grateful for the U.S. embassy and all the rescue efforts.
And I hope that, you know, all the different countries, governments, people of different cultures and faiths and all come together and do everything to get the communications back and provide support to the people affected.
Thank you so much for having me on.
WHITFIELD: Very important. Thank you so much, Ramya.
TADIPATRI: Thank you.
WHITFIELD: And of course, for more information, you know, that you all could benefit from on how you can help the Nepal flood victims go to CNN.com/impact.

BARBIE LATZA NADEAU, CNN CONTRIBUTOR: Yes, you know, we know that that search and rescue operation is still ongoing but night is falling in this part of the world and so that's going to be very complicated.

This was not a car ferry. So there's no complication of vehicles. This was a passenger ferry, but it was leaving a very popular holiday island of Cyprus, heading back to Turkey when it started to take on water in bad weather.
Now we're getting some detail about the survivors, those people who did get off that vessel said that it was a harrowing situation, that it was very quick.
Many of those were rescued by helicopters, some by rescue boats, some by even private vessels brought to the shore back in Cyprus. And the seas were incredibly rough at the time they were -- they were rescued.
But there'll be a lot of questions asked about just why this happened. This should be a very stable ferry and the Mediterranean -- while the seas were rough, there were no warnings that should have not allowed them to take off from the port there in Cyprus.
So there will be questions to ask. We aren't getting a lot of information about the nationalities of these people. We know that seven bodies have been recovered. We know that 23 people at this hour are still missing, and they're very likely trying to search for those people who are still inside that ferry as its overturned, making it very difficult this time of the night as darkness falls, Fredricka.
WHITFIELD: Yes, incredible. Barbie Latza Nadeau, thank you so much. Keep us posted as you learn more.
Coming up, more than 900 workers among the missing in the Nepal floods trapped inside power plant tunnels. We'll hear from the lucky survivors who made it out about their terrifying and hellish escape. That's next.

All right. Sir Anthony Hopkins is one of the most celebrated actors of our time, with a career spanning more than six decades in film, television and theater. But he is also passionate about another art form, and he's launching a music career at the tender age of 88.
Our Elex Michaelson sat down with him for details.
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
ELEX MICHAELSON, CNN CORRESPONDENT (voice-over): Sir Anthony Hopkins is listening to a performance of music he composed.
SIR ANTHONY HOPKINS, COMPOSER, "LIFE IS A DREAM": I'm sitting there thinking, where did that come from?
MICHAELSON: Where did it come from?
HOPKINS: From inside my soul.
Closer.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Hopkins is perhaps best known for playing the cannibalistic killer Hannibal Lecter in several movies starting with "Silence of the Lambs".
[14:45:05]
HOPKINS AS HANNIBAL LECTER: A census taker once tried to test me. I ate his liver with some fava beans and a nice chianti.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Real life Hopkins just released his first ever album of his own classical compositions.
MICHAELSON: Do you think Hannibal Lecter would like this album?
(LAUGHTER)
HOPKINS: It's a silly -- that's a question. Hello Clarice. I like the Clarice -- the Clarice aria.
(LAUGHTER)
MICHAELSON (voice-over): The album is titled "Life is a Dream".
MICHAELSON: What does "Life is a Dream" mean?
HOPKINS: Exactly that. Life is a dream. That's as I see it now, at 88- 1/2 years of age, going on to my 90s. I just see it as one a long dream. I look back over my life. I've met impossible. How did I get here?
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Hopkins grew up as an only child in Wales. He'll never forget when his parents took him to see the classical music star Jose Iturbi.
HOPKINS: I went backstage and got his autograph, but that's where my love of music started.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): His favorite musical performance still bring Tony to tears.
HOPKINS: I can't even talk about it without getting choked up.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): By age seven, Hopkins was playing piano. By 15, he taught himself how to write his own music. But he didn't want to publish it.
HOPKINS: I was cynical.
MICHAELSON: You didn't think people would like it?
HOPKINS: I didn't believe in myself at all. That's why I became an actor.
(LAUGHTER)
MICHAELSON (voice-over): The legendary actor Sir Laurence Olivier believed in Hopkins acting ability and became his lifelong mentor.
HOPKINS: I think he was taken by my pugnacious strength. I was a bit of a rebel.
MICHAELSON: Yeah.
HOPKINS: Bad boy.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): That bad boy would become one of the most celebrated actors of all time.
He'd win an Oscar for "Silence of the Lambs" and another for "The Father".
HOPKINS: I don't know what's happening anymore.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): He'd earn Oscar nominations for "The Remains of the Day", "Amistad," "Nixon", and "The Two Popes". But through it all, he never stopped writing music.
MICHAELSON: Is more of your passion than even acting?
HOPKINS: Yeah, more so now than ever.
MICHAELSON: Yeah.
HOPKINS: But it's been in me.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Hopkins' wife, Stella, encouraged him to compile his seven decades worth of songwriting into one album.
MICHAELSON: Is there something, though, about it's never too late to try something new to.
HOPKINS: Never too late. And realization that this is it. So, we better wake up and live.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Fellow actor Bradley Cooper suggested to Hopkins that he team up with world-renowned maestro Gustavo Dudamel. Dudamel taught Cooper how to conduct for his role in "Maestro". Dudamel is now preparing to lead the New York Philharmonic. He recently conducted the halftime show at the World Cup final.
HOPKINS: Hey, congratulations, how are you?
MICHAELSON (voice-over): This is the first time these two have ever done a TV interview together.
Dudamel recalling the first time he read Hopkins sheet music.
GUSTAVO DUDAMEL, COMPOSER: And I was digesting all of that inside of me, all the sound. I was completely in love immediately. I say like wow.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Soon afterwards, Dudamel agreed to conduct the recording sessions in London.
HOPKINS: I'm sitting thinking, did I write that?
(LAUGHTER)
DUDAMEL: Yes, you did.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): They performed their album live in Saudi Arabia.
We met up with them at the Grammy Museum in Los Angeles shortly before they appeared on stage together.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Congratulations on this new album, "Life is a Dream".
MICHAELSON (voice-over): The two discussing the album's tracks, which are largely inspired by Hopkins childhood. They play the music for the audience.
MICHAESLON: How would you describe Sir Anthony Hopkins, the composer?
DUDAMEL: The most generous person.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Dudamel says Hopkins' pure joy for living, evidenced by all the dancing videos he posts to social media, is inspiring.
DUDAMEL: The most honest thing, I believe, is when somebody opens his heart, his soul, and he shares that with you. This is generosity. Welcome to the Hollywood bowl.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): Dudamel just wrapped up his 17-year run leading the L.A. Philharmonic at the Hollywood Bowl.
His final weekend, including a performance with the Foo Fighters.
DUDAMEL: A life-changing experience.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): And a collaboration with Coldplay's Chris Martin, raising money for earthquake victims in Dudamel's native Venezuela.
DUDAMEL: Music is not only entertainment. Music heals, you know, and it heals the spirit.
[14:50:03]
And we need more of that.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): A share of the proceeds from "Life is a Dream" will support earthquake relief in Venezuela.
MICHAELSON: Why was that important for you?
HOPKINS: Well, it was such a tragedy. And, my friend Venezuelan. And then the opportunity came. Well, put all that in into the relief fund.
MICHAELSON (voice-over): In less than a year, Hopkins and Dudamel have become good friends, two men from different generations and different continents united by the universal language of music, something they both learned to love as little boys.
HOPKINS: We never grew up.
MICHAELSON: What do you mean by that?
HOPKINS: We're both kids.
MICHAELSON: Yeah.
HOPKINS: We're both children.
MICHAELSON: Even at 88.
HOPKINS: Yes. He wasn't grown up watching on the podium conducting the other night. He's a child. That's what it's about, about the power of childhood.
MICHAELSON: Wow.
HOPKINS: The child of enthusiasm.
DUDAMEL: Absolutely.
HOPKINS: Passion. MICHAELSON (voice-over): Elex Michaelson, CNN, Los Angeles.
(END VIDEOTAPE)

JOHN U. BACON, AUTHOR: It's USC, UCLA, it's Michigan, Ohio State, Alabama, Auburn, Texas, Oklahoma. These teams never move. They never change colors. They never say, we're going to move to Nashville for a better stadium. Not one college team has ever left their town. It is truly a deeply regional game.
JESSICA MENDOZA, SPORTS BROADCASTER AND ANALYST, ESPN: I think until you're in those environments, you just don't understand, like this is a way of life. It's not just like, hey, I dig some football. Let's go watch. No, no, no, no. Like we hate them.
BACON: Were people getting tired of in the '60s, '70s and '80s was mythical national titles. You'd have 2 or 3 undefeated teams. They didn't play each other. Pick one. It's a beauty contest. VAN LATHAN, JOURNALIST: You play all your regular season games and
then you get invited to these gigantic bowls, Orange Bowl, Sugar Bowl, Fiesta Bowl, Rose Bowl, and you play against these other teams. And then somebody would sit back and they would vote on which team they thought was the best.
Well, this became so controversial that they said, we can no longer trust the human eye to tell us what the best team is.
The Bowl Championship Series, the BCS was created to crown the number one and number two teams based upon a computer scoring system, and then those two teams would face off for the national championship.
BACON: The BCS is a semi solution where at least the top two we think are going to play each other. That settles part of it. But you still have some problems there.
LATHAN: College football is very regional and so you have different play styles from all over different places. There were some people that thought the computer could not deal with the nuance of this. And so, you got matchups sometimes in the BCS between teams that people could not still agree on.
That is why people decided, you know what, we are going to just make a four-team playoff, and then we're going to expand that playoff to try to come closer to something that resembles a tournament for the national championship in college football.

The frantic search and rescue efforts on the Nepali-Chinese border are now in their fourth day, following those catastrophic floods that washed away entire villages and towns under debris and mud. Today, the death toll has risen to more than 780 people, some 3,000 are still missing, including almost 1,000 workers at various hydropower projects in Nepal. Emergency workers are now pumping air and dropping food into one tunnel, but have yet to establish communication with anyone inside.
Today, displaced survivors are facing new concerns after a landslide on Saturday formed another barrier lake that officials fear could lead to more flooding. Help continues to arrive in the flood zone. A shipment from China has landed in Kathmandu, and the U.S. is sending more than $3.5 million of aid.

hear stories from people who just barely survived this massive flood, perhaps by just seconds.

The frantic search and rescue efforts on the Nepali-Chinese border are now in their fourth day following those catastrophic floods that washed away entire villages and sank towns under debris and mud.

CNN's Will Ripley joins us now from Nepal with the latest.

WILL RIPLEY, CNN SENIOR INTERNATIONAL CORRESPONDENT: So we continue to hear stories from people who just barely survived this massive flood, perhaps by just seconds. RIPLEY: Did you know it was water that was coming when you saw the big black --

RIPLEY: And you said the healing power of the Nepali people has kept you here and helped you to heal from this experience.
LUTUMOLO: We were together. They were not different between Italians, Nepali -- we were just human beings, survivor. And we just try the night time celebrating life and they lost everything. They lost everything -- people, houses they have nothing. They had nothing left.

DAVID FISHER, HEAD OF THE NEPAL DELEGATION, INTERNATIONAL FEDERATION OF RED CROSS: Well, we've been able to reach some of the people who have been affected in Nuwakot and in Dhading districts and many of them are desperate. Still, they ve escaped with nothing but the clothes on their backs. Many are children who have lost their parents, many are older people who have lost their families.
They also are happy to be alive, but they need everything. And so the Nepal Red Cross has worked with authorities to find temporary shelter for them and religious buildings and community buildings, very temporary setting for them.
And the really good news is that a road has been opened now between, Nuwakot and Rasuwa, which is the area that was hardest hit by the flood and has been completely cut off from road traffic and has only been accessed by helicopter until now.
So our team from the red cross spent 12 bumpy hours to get through there today and we will start doing assessments and try to bring in some additional aid.
The Armed Forces have been doing rescue, bringing in some aid, but there is so much more that's needed.
WHITFIELD: Yes, so starting with that temporary shelter that you described, in some cases, you're able to create something like that in some of these buildings. Paint a picture. What does it look like?

[15:05:10]
I mean, bring us there.
FISHER: Yes, so we provided this sort of very thin mats, a little bit like yoga mats and with blankets and hygiene kits and we are able to provide hot food and a couple of items here and there, but it is not much to be honest. There are hundreds of people like that even in Bidur and what is going to be important is that we start to register these people and provide them not just these items, but, you know, cash, so that they can go and buy what they need and travel if they need to and make their own solutions.
But what is most pressing also are all the people who haven't received much of anything yet in the areas that were inaccessible and that is why we are so happy now that there is at least a road, even if it is very difficult to pass.
WHITFIELD: My goodness. David, earlier the director of a water aid agency told CNN that there is an urgent need for clean water in the area. Are you hearing that as well? And what can be done? What are the options?
FISHER: It is absolutely true. So, in the first instance, we just have to bring in bottled water, but soon that has to be replaced by something that's more manageable, large bladders of water and as soon as things -- we can very rapidly fix the water sites here so that people can have access to clean water. But if they don t, if they start using, you know, flood waters, there is a very, very high risk of disease.
All right, I mean, so many, you know, potential dangers still existing while the search is ongoing for potential survivors or even in the recovery efforts.
David Fisher in Kathmandu, thank you so much.
All the best in your continued endeavors.

And so Nancy Grace Roman, the woman herself was the first astronomer, chief astronomer at NASA. She was the first executive at NASA and she was very influential in the design of space telescopes like this one.
So when you get up above the Earth's atmosphere, way, way out in space, you can get a much clearer view of the cosmos and the couple of things that these scientists, researchers are trying to determine is what is the source of gravity that we detect among galaxies in the universe? That is to us, invisible and it goes by the charming phrase "dark matter" and along with that is dark energy.
We can't readily detect it, but we can see its effects. So you ve got to figure if it is going on in the deep cosmos, its going on here and we don t quite understand it. That's the first thing.
The second thing is this telescope is so -- it captures so much light so quickly that altogether you get about a thousand times the observing power of the Hubble Space Telescope, which in itself is very successful.
And so we hope to find more evidence of black holes, very massive black holes and then exoplanets. These are planets that would be orbiting other stars. And even these unusual things that go by the term "rogue planets" that seem to be adrift or following space time gravitational pathways between stars. It is really extraordinary.

NYE: Well, I mean, I want to find evidence of life on another world and so people throw around the phrase "biosignature," where you detect evidence of life on another world. That would be extraordinary. But it might give everybody a nudge that is to say, change the course of history.

New today voters in Iceland have rejected a nationwide referendum to resume talks to join the European Union. The debate centered on national identity, the economy and protecting the country's fishing rights. Those in favor said E.U. membership could offer more security, especially against President Trump's threats to take over nearby Greenland.
Iceland first applied to join the E.U. back in 2009, in the wake of a major financial crisis. Now political uncertainty, including U.S. tariffs and fighting in the Middle East, have renewed the debate.

(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)

He was processed far more quickly than many people facing deportation. White House border czar, Tom Homan was asked about that today on CNN's "State of the Union."
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
DANA BASH, CNN ANCHOR AND POLITICAL CORRESPONDENT: Was he given preferential treatment to leave instead of remaining in an ICE detention facility longer?

The quicker when a judge orders them out, rather than being in detention waiting for travel documents and flight arrangements. ICE, along with CBP through the Department of Homeland Security is working really good on the efficiencies of the removal process.

(END VIDEO CLIP)
WHITFIELD: Homan said political affiliations are not a factor in deportation, and that the goal is to remove anyone who is ordered deported as quickly as possible.
The Trump administration is scaling up its immigration enforcement operations at the nation's airports, and it is now targeting a growing number of people who thought they were allowed to remain in the United States while their asylum applications are pending.
Shimon Prokupecz has the harrowing story of one woman who was arrested at the airport.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
UNIDENTIFIED FEMALE: We all know this is unfair.
VELTIA: It is unfair and you know that.
SHIMON PROKUPECZ, CNN SENIOR CRIME AND JUSTICE CORRESPONDENT (voice over): We were with Indi Veltia the day she went to have an electronic monitor placed on her ankle. It was days after she was released from ICE detention.
PROKUPECZ (on camera): I saw how upset you were when you came out.
VELTIA: You see a monitor and you think you're looking at the person that is a criminal. And I am not a criminal. PROKUPECZ (voice over): Veltia is seeking asylum in the United States. She and her wife left Venezuela seven years ago, and in that time, she says she has done everything the U.S. government has asked of her. Then one day, coming home from a business trip.
VELTIA: The airline called me to the counter and say they needed to balance the weight on the aircraft, and they were moving me to the front rows. Again, at this point, it was normal.
[15:35:10]
And then when I was boarding and I was getting into the jet ramp -- the bridge, these, I will say four or six guys came out of nowhere and like in plain clothes and pushed me to a wall and at this point I saw that I was being assaulted.
I asked, am I being detained? For what? And he only said yes and never said why I was being detained. So, they handcuffed me.
PROKUPECZ (voice over): The 47-year-old and her wife live with their dogs in Indianapolis, where she has worked her way up from a warehouse assistant to her corporate role, where she is known for making valuable efficiencies, ultimately saving her company thousands of dollars her coworkers tell CNN.
That she says as part of her asylum application, which was filed in 2019, Immigration officials previously told her, "You may remain in the U.S. until your asylum application is decided." She is still waiting to be called for an interview.
After her arrest at Atlanta's airport, it would be hours before anyone heard from her.
VELTIA: I was begging to call -- to call my wife to let her know what was happening. I was on a business trip, to call my boss. They finally allowed me to have that call. They said I had five minutes, so I called my wife first. Quick one. The toughest call I had to make my entire life.
I called my boss and then I called my best friend to let her know what was happening and to ask for help.
PROKUPECZ (voice over): She was taken to a detention center in Lumpkin, Georgia, where she stayed for 21 days. The experience changed her.
VELTIA: When we got there, there was the most inhumane treatment. They led us in a room that I will say it was around 50 degrees. I was in t- shirt. They finally moved me to a cell at 11:00 A.M. the next day, so it was 14 hours in that hostile environment at 50 degrees.
And they finally, at around 3:00 A.M., someone came to the door and I begged, if they can give me a jacket, which they finally did. They give you underwear, three uniforms, of course, that many people have used before. And a sheet that also you can see through. The only way to drink water is through the faucet on the sink. And -- but they don t give you cups. So you have to find your way to drink water.
And also, they don t give you any utensils when they bring food.
PROKUPECZ (on camera): Did they ever ask you if you wanted to waive and just leave?
VELTIA: They say, we pay you $2,600.00 if you leave. Do you want voluntary departure?
PROKUPECZ: What did you do? How did you get through it?
VELTIA: What kept me sane? The love of my community. I talked to my girls daily and they were -- I am sorry -- they were reading the letters that all of my coworkers had for me and that is what kept me sane because I thought that I was going to lose my mind.
I never thought that I could experience something like this. In a country where you believe that the people do the things by the book, I am a rule follower. I always have been.
I have done everything that the government has asked me to do. And anyway, I was detained like a criminal. I am broken. That's how I feel.
PROKUPECZ (voice over): After nearly a month and dozens of letters, friends and coworkers sent to the judge, Veltia was released on a $15,000.00 bond, money her friends scraped together.
VELTIA: Those centers are there to break you down and you decide to go. Even if your life is in danger, if you go to your country that you would rather be out of those centers. Those centers are built to break you down.
I don t think i want to be ever again in none of those centers.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: What's going on?
[15:40:10]
PROKUPECZ (voice over): What happened to Veltia is playing out all across the country.
UNIDENTIFIED FEMALE: Where are you? Help! Help!
PROKUPECZ (voice over): Increasingly, people with papers they thought allowed them to be in the United States are being targeted.
JORGE GAVILLANES, IMMIGRATION ATTORNEY, PARTNER KUCK BAXTER: Ive been practicing immigration for more than ten years. Ive never seen this level of enforcement. Weve been describing the immigration system as broken for decades. Now, it is just complete chaos.
PROKUPECZ (voice over): The Department of Homeland Security tells CNN, this administration is working diligently to ensure that aliens in our country illegally can no longer fly unless it is out of our country to self-deport.
To be clear, they say, a pending application does not confer legal status in the United States.
GAVILLANES: In fact, the government has given them a work permit to work, get a driver's license, and do whatever they need to do to take care of themselves while their application is pending.
But now, the same government is telling them, oh no, you're not allowed to be here, which is contradictory.
PROKUPECZ (on camera): These ramped up operations across U.S. airports by ICE officers even got an American citizen caught up.
JOHN PHILLIPS, DETAINED BY ICE: I was just living my life kind of living in my little white guy world. Three guys jump out at me. I said, I am an American citizen. I am like, I am totally shocked now that this has happened. I am just like, holy smokes! There is a lot of bad stuff happening.
We have an agency of our government who is completely unfettered and unaccountable to anybody.
PROKUPECZ (voice over): Phillips was released after ICE realized he was the wrong person.
For Veltia, she is still grappling with what happened to her and adjusting to her new normal.
VELTIA: I won't let them give them the credit to keep me broken. I will rebuild myself and I will for sure grow up stronger.
PROKUPECZ (on camera): And Indi told me that she was scared to come forward, scared that by coming forward and telling her story and telling people what she went through, that somehow there would be some kind of retribution.
But she felt it was important for people to know what she has been through.
Shimon Prokupecz, CNN, New York.
(END VIDEOTAPE)

UNIDENTIFIED MALE: It's good that you came. This is the time where the Crow Fair celebration is happening.
RIPLEY (voice over): For over 100 years, a temporary city pops up every summer in the shadow of the Big Horn Mountains. This is Crow Fair, known as the "Teepee Capital of the World," with 1,500 canvases raised on the Crow reservation outside of Billings, Montana.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Bingo.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: We've been using teepees as far back as probably like 1600s, 1700s. You feel alive, you know, you feel like you're like a real Indian.
RIPLEY (voice over): Today, this is one of the largest gatherings of Native Americans in North America. Indigenous communities and tourists come from around the world to experience it. The day starts with a parade in Crow Regalia.
VANESSA AFRAID OF BEAR, MISS CROW NATION: One of the biggest topics in Indian country today is the loss of culture. With Crow Fair being as big as it is, it kind of aids us in the way that we're able to keep our teachings and our traditions.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Now here they come.
RIPLEY (voice over): One of those traditions is the adrenaline filled Indian relay. It's a race where riders jump off a bareback horse onto another.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Now this is an outstanding team.
AFRAID OF BEAR: One common misconception is people believe that they need to be invited. But really, the invitation is open ended. I just think that the best way to support us is just being here. This is my invitation. This is me inviting you.

All right, experts are often warning that A.I. could wipe out huge numbers of jobs in the future, but not for this California barber who is leaning in and helping to teach artificial intelligence how to cut hair.
The Bay Area barber wears five cameras mounted on his head, chest and wrists. The footage he collects is used to mimic his movements and map angles of his hands to teach robots how to move and work.
[15:50:09]
He says he is personally not worried about A.I. because the technology cannot replace close, personal connections.
All right, coming up, for more than 130 years, these temple doors have been closed to nonmembers. CNN gets a rare look inside one of the most sacred places in the Latter Day Saints faith ahead of public tours.

(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
WATT (voice over): For roughly 17 million devotees worldwide, this is the holiest of holies where sacred covenants are made with God.

So why am I allowed now? Well, because they are opening this temple up for the world to see. This used to be an historic temple.
WATT (on camera): Why are you letting us in?

WATT (voice over): Why now? Well, as you can see, the temple is being renovated. We've been allowed to take a peek.

WATT: So these are the dressing rooms where church members change into their all white attire before going into the temple. Now all of this construction is new, made to look old. Look, if you take a look at that chandelier, that is modeled on a chandelier from a catalogue from the time that the old temple was built.
WATT (voice over): Construction on the Salt Lake Temple began in 1853.
Oh, how construction technology has changed.
KIRBY: We are under the temple, so the stone walls of the temple are right above us here.
WATT (voice over): Nearly 200 million pounds of temple now resting on 98 earthquake proofing base isolators.
WATT (on camera): How much movement does this give you?
KIRBY: Five-feet in any direction.

KIRBY: Not at all. I believe, I've been prepared by God to be on this project.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: The Salt Lake Temple you see outside is called the House of the Lord.

JOHNSON: A husband and a wife, a man and a woman kneel across an altar with their hounds interlocked, and they're sealed not just for time, but for time and eternity.
WATT (voice over): And you'll be able to see the baptistery.

KIRBY: Yes.

UNIDENTIFIED MALE: We want to help you feel comfortable about what we're doing.

## [03] Italian Tourists Narrowly Survive Nepal Flash Flood; At Least 85 Americans Among Those Unaccounted For In Floods; U.S. Strikes Iranian Island In Strait Of Hormuz; Jurors Resume Deliberations In Lindsay Clancy Case On Monday; Silent Mental Health Crisis Inside The U.S. Navy; Military Mental Health; Norfolk State Marching Band; Apocalypse Hideaway

[16:00:00]
FREDRICKA WHITFIELD, CNN HOST: We continue tracking the latest breaking developments on the desperate search and rescue efforts underway on the Nepali-Chinese border, following those catastrophic floods.
Today, the death toll has risen to more than 780 people. Some 3,000 remain missing, including almost 1,000 workers at various hydropower projects in Nepal. Emergency workers are now pumping air and dropping food into one tunnel, but have yet to establish communication with anyone inside.
And today, displaced survivors are facing new concerns after a landslide on Saturday formed another barrier lake that officials fear could lead to more flooding.
CNN's Will Ripley is in Nepal and joins us with the latest on the search and rescue efforts.
WILL RIPLEY, CNN SENIOR INTERNATIONAL CORRESPONDENT: So we continue to hear stories from people who just barely survived this massive flood, perhaps by just seconds. And one of the couples that I spoke with today is an Italian couple who actually were at that border checkpoint that saw some of the most severe damage. They saw tour groups, other tour buses full of people essentially just wiped away.
And we're now learning that bodies have been turning up sometimes hundreds of miles from the initial spot where they were initially washed away, just to show you the strength and the violence of the water. Authorities also believe there are many bodies yet to be discovered that are buried beneath multiple layers of quicksand all along the flood route. So it's a very grim situation with so many people, 3,000 people missing.
But there are some here who believe that the number of missing could actually be far higher than that because a lot of cases have been so far unreported. But I want you to hear about this couple's story. Stefano and Valentina, they talked to me earlier about how they managed to survive.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
VALENTINA PICCININI, ITALIAN FLOOD SURVIVOR: Everybody turned right, only me turned left. So that's the point. I don't know why, but in my mind, I can -- I don't know, I cannot explain. I cannot see anything, anybody around me. I cannot hear my name because they are only -- everybody is saying my name. But I was not able to hear them. So I just following Hernan and he showed me the only way I could follow to survive. The only way. Because if I'm going straight on, for sure I would not be here.
RIPLEY: Did you know it was water that was coming when you saw the big black --
STEFANO LUTUMOLO, ITALIAN FLOOD SURVIVOR: No, we couldn't know. Like I have seen the immigration point. It was the beginning. And we just saw something crashing into buses 30 meters in front of us. It was very big like it was crazy. The sound, it was unbelievable.
PICCININI: I felt that everybody of them, they were alive. I don't know why, but my heart can tell me, OK, don't worry. They are all alive. So first, start to think about you, start to run. Think on survive. And then somebody somehow, universe. God, Shiva, whoever, will find a way to bring you back to them or bring them to you.
RIPLEY: And you said the healing power of the Nepali people has kept you here and helped you heal from this experience.
LUTUMOLO: We were together. They were not different between Italians, Nepali. We were just human beings, survivor, and we just try. Also the night time celebrating life. And they lost everything. They lost everything. People, houses. They have nothing. They had nothing left.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
RIPLEY: And, Fred, they know they are the lucky ones when there are just so many people and so many families who are wondering what happened to their loved ones who were also lost in this flood and have yet to be seen or heard from.
WHITFIELD: Will Ripley, thank you so much.
Among the missing at least 85 Americans. The U.S. State Department says nine Americans have been safely evacuated.
CNN's Rafael Romo is following this for us and with me now.
What are you learning?
RAFAEL ROMO, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Well, there are so many families who are desperate for any news, really, from their loved ones who are still missing there in the disaster area. And, Fred, over the last 24 hours, we've been able to get in touch with some of the families of a group of men traveling together for a hiking trip of a lifetime, for both spiritual and recreational reasons.
There were nine Americans and one Canadian in the missing group of friends, traveling as part of a tour as catastrophic flash flooding hit the area where they were around the Nepal-China border on Wednesday. One of those missing man is Kesava Rao Tadipatri, a 73- year-old American father who lives in Arizona. Tadipatri is the one located in the upper left corner of this picture, holding a phone.
And, Fred, you know this because you spoke with his daughter in the last couple of hours. And earlier today, she told you that her father sent his family the group photo of himself and his fellow hikers just a short time before the flood waters hit the border crossing.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
RAMYA TADIPATRI, DAUGHTER OF MISSING MAN IN NEPAL: That picture that you showed was the -- we had received at 8:36.
[16:05:02]
And the tremors, the first tremors we understand, happened at 8:37, then at 8:42 was -- we received a message from my dad and he said I will have some issues near the Trishuli River, which was that river that was flooded. And my mom asked what issues and then it was a communication blackout that was the last we heard.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: Tadipatri described her father as a spiritual guru and guide for the Hindu community and shared with CNN that the 73-year-old had run his second marathon earlier this year.
CNN also got in touch with the wife of another member of the same group of men hiking together near the Nepal-China border when tragedy struck. Sneha Sudhir says her husband, Srinivas Sudhir Rangu, was enjoying a long anticipated hiking trip through Mount Kailash with nine other family friends.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
SNEHA SUDHIR, WIFE OF MAN MISSING IN NEPAL: He was on a hiking trip from Nepal. And then he was with nine other friends, family friends. It was supposed to be a simple two weeks' Nepal hiking trip where he would go, have some good time with his boys, and be back home.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: Sudhir also told us the hikers, including her husband, had been preparing for months for this trip, and the men, whom she described as great human beings, were all in good physical shape.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
SUDHIR: So these boys were supposed to be going on a hike. And then that was starting from Nepal. And eventually would be the Mount Kailash trek and be back home. That was their plan. And they had a wonderful guide. They had prepared for this particular hike for close to about three months.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: Meanwhile, help continues to arrive in the flood zone. A shipment from China just landed in Kathmandu, and the United States is sending more than $3.5 million of additional aid. But just your heart breaks for these families.
WHITFIELD: It does.
ROMO: Especially just considering that they haven't received any news since Wednesday.
WHITFIELD: Nothing. Yes. All right. Well, we and everybody continue to hope and pray for them.
ROMO: Yes.
WHITFIELD: Rafael Romo, thank you so much.
All right. And this is how you can help. Scan the QR code at the bottom of your screen or text the word flood to 707070 to donate.

WHITFIELD: All right, more breaking news. The U.S. military has launched new strikes on Iran in the Strait of Hormuz, the first strikes in over a month now.
Let's go to the White House. Julia Benbrook is there.
Julia, what are you learning?
JULIA BENBROOK, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Well, we are learning that earlier today, U.S. forces struck two Iranian launchers on Lauric Island. A U.S. official tells us that this took place after Islamic Revolutionary Guard forces were observed preparing rockets with sea mines to launch at the Strait of Hormuz. That critical waterway, of course, has been a key area of focus during this now more than six- month long conflict.
Now, this official went on to say that U.S. forces are monitoring the area closely and remain prepared to protect the free flow of commerce through this essential waterway. And Trump has previously said that there would be no hesitation when it comes to the U.S. military shooting down boats, laying mines in the Strait of Hormuz.
Now, it was just last week that Trump and his administration made it clear that they wanted to ramp up economic pressure on Iran. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent had referred to it as economic D-Day, and they did announce plans to put pressure on Iran and countries doing business with Iran.
We're somewhat vague in the rollout of those plans, but they've made it clear that that's how they want to act going forward is by putting this economic -- this financial pressure on Iran. And all of this is taking place as poll after poll has shown that the majority of Americans do not approve of the way that Trump has handled this conflict so far -- Fred.
WHITFIELD: All right. Julia Benbrook, we'll check back with you. Thank you so much.
All right. Coming up, we're taking you inside the 50-year-old legacy of Norfolk State's Spartan Legion, one of the most iconic HBCU bands in the country.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[16:19:08]
WHITFIELD: Deliberations in the Lindsay Clancy triple murder trial are set to resume tomorrow morning. The former Massachusetts nurse, who tried to take her own life after she killed her three children, pleaded not guilty to three counts of murder. Clancy was prescribed about a dozen different medications in 2022 and 2023 to treat her mental illnesses and disorders. Her defense team argues she is not criminally responsible because she was suffering from postpartum psychosis.
I want to bring in now criminal defense attorney Elyse Hershon for more legal perspective.
Elise, great to see you. So jurors began deliberations on Thursday, deliberating three hours that day, all day Friday, and then now resuming on Monday. So what do you think they've been assessing all weekend long?
ELYSE HERSHON, CRIMINAL DEFENSE ATTORNEY: Good afternoon, and thank you for having me back on your show. So essentially we know that they're looking at the physical evidence because on Friday they asked to see two pieces of evidence, a knife and a bottle or some bottles of pills that they made a big thing about during the trial.
[16:20:11]
So that shows that they're taking it seriously. Thursday likely, there's over 200, 300 exhibits they probably were organizing for a lot of that time. And then Friday they hunkered down and started going through it. This really all comes down to state of mind, but they do have to go back and see what physical evidence supports and whether the prosecution has proved that.
WHITFIELD: Right. Now this was not a sequestered jury either. So they're able to go home, you know, of course, you know, instructions from the court. Not likely, you know, allowed to read or even communicate with anybody about this case. But, you know, does this kind of time individually for two days allow them to better assess things? Do they, you know, come back together on Monday and kind of share thoughts and, you know, as they resume deliberations?
HERSHON: It's a great question. Essentially this time over the weekend, you know, they're instructed not to go on social media, not to discuss the case, but they can't shut their brain off and their thoughts off. So sometimes over the weekend, they can either affirm the position they were taking or going towards the -- on the Friday before, or maybe they start to see another point of view and they're into more of a compromising mindset when they come back Monday.
We've seen like in several murder trial murder trials, in the Brian Walshe case, they come back on a Monday morning with a verdict. So it is possible they come back tomorrow refreshed and ready to make a decision.
WHITFIELD: As you reflect on this case, were there any particular testimonies that you think were, you know, particularly resonating that you think were persuasive either side?
HERSHON: This is -- how much time do I have? Yes. So the first witness is the husband. And I think this, as the trial went on, we realized that Patrick Clancy's testimony had a lot of inconsistencies in it. But strategically, I think the prosecution put him on first because as they called the rest of the witnesses, then they couldn't go back and say, well, how come your testimony doesn't line up with this?
He was kind of one in and one out, and he went back out of state to New York. So he really stood out. And as the expert testimonies came at the end, three rebuttal witnesses from the prosecution, three on the defense side, I think it was really telling. They all recognized that Lindsay Clancy had mental illness that she was suffering, but to what degree. The three prosecution experts say, no, she is criminally responsible. She knew what she was doing. She could have stopped herself. She did plan this and made decisions along the way.
And the defense witnesses saying, and, you know, one of them having worked with Jeffrey Dahmer, very, very well-reputed, world renowned, and they said, no, she's not criminally responsible. So it's up to the jury who they credit. I thought the defense witnesses were very credible. But remember, the prosecution is required to prove this beyond a reasonable doubt. If you're weighing two sides, that's the definition of reasonable doubt.
WHITFIELD: OK. All jurors have to, you know, be in agreement with the outcome. But if it's a hung jury, where would this case, in your view, go?
HERSHON: I think all sides would be frustrated with a hung jury. And if there was a mistrial declared, it's so much work, so much emotion that went into this case. So essentially the prosecution can decide to try this case over and over again until they get to a verdict. We saw it with Karen Read.
They tried that case again and actually lost public support for that prosecution as it went along. And I imagine if they decided to retry this case, I think the global support for this would be even more eye opening and, and they should really decide not to re-prosecute.
She is either going to end up in a psychiatric hospital for the foreseeable future or state prison if they decide on a verdict here. Very unlikely they acquit her all across the board. So it's really just a question of where is she going to go after this.
WHITFIELD: Wow. All right. Elyse Hershon, appreciate you. Thank you so much.
HERSHON: Thank you.
WHITFIELD: Coming up, the silent mental health crisis inside the U.S. Navy and the culture that keeps so many sailors from getting help.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
JORDAN HOUGH, FORMER NAVY ENLISTED SAILOR: I think there are so many more people struggling than we realize because they're afraid to speak up for whatever reason it may be. Maybe it's pride. Maybe it's fear of repercussions. You know, whatever it may be. And that's sad.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[16:29:10]
WHITFIELD: The USS Abraham Lincoln is expected to stop in Thailand this week as part of its voyage home to San Diego. A Thai Defense Ministry spokesperson said the crew will be able to, quote, "go ashore and relax," end quote. Earlier this month, the carrier came under scrutiny after reports of mental health challenges on board while in the Middle East.
But as CNN's Pamela Brown reports, the toll of extended deployments is not unique to the Lincoln or its mission.
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
HOUGH: I don't want to be looked down on. I don't want a bad reputation. I don't want to let down the team.
PAMELA BROWN, CNN CHIEF INVESTIGATIVE CORRESPONDENT (voice-over): When Jordan Hough, a former Navy enlisted sailor, felt his mental health began to spiral in 2024 while deployed on an aircraft carrier, speaking up and asking for help wasn't easy.
HOUGH: Oh, yes, there's a stigma for sure. And it's sad because I don't really think that it should be.
BROWN (voice-over): Hough says he sought counseling even before deployment under the crashing demands of getting his nuclear qualifications and caring for his dying mom.
[16:30:09]
HOUGH: When I was transferring for my last bit of training to my ship in San Diego is when my mother passed away. And it made everything more difficult, as you can imagine.
BROWN (on camera): You had to leave her when she was in the hospital, right?
HOUGH: I had to leave my mother on her deathbed in the hospital to go report for my new duty station, which is awful.
BROWN (voice-over): He says he boarded the USS Abraham Lincoln in May of 2024 with the knowledge he needed for the job as a nuclear engineer, but not the mental preparation for what life would actually be like.
HOUGH: I was surrounded by metal walls everywhere, tight corridors, and the amount of people on that boat. Gosh, it was crowded. Not to mention the long waits for the chow line. It's something that maybe I've -- I knew or have heard of before but had no idea what to really expect, until I was there.
BROWN: Two months later, he reached out for help.
HOUGH: I was prescribed some medication on the ship. I was very happy to be receiving some help. I don't recall if the medication was working for me or not. However, while out at sea that the ship ran out of supply, they didn't have enough medication to give to me. I just switched my medications mid deployment.
BROWN: And despite that, he says there were plenty of mental health resources on board with counselors, chaplains, and even a therapy dog. But underlying it all was a bigger problem with the culture around mental health.
HOUGH: When I started seeking help, it disqualified me from my nuclear work aboard the ship, so I couldn't really participate in group work. And I was definitely looked down on by my peers for that. A feeling or vibe like, oh, this guy doesn't want to work. He's just going to pretend to be sad to get out of it.
And so, I think I looked -- I was looked at as lazy for that. When, in fact, I was really just trying to get some sort of help.
BROWN (on camera): And you were suicidal?
HOUGH: Unfortunately, yes. At that time, I was -- I was suicidal.
BROWN (voice-over): Jordan's experience is part of a much larger national problem in the military. A 2021 study, published by the National Center for Biotechnology information, found that 23 percent of active duty service members were depressed. That's compared to an estimated 13.1 percent of the general population being diagnosed with depression.
TERI CASERTA: I believe that if they actually got him to mental health when he was begging for it, he'd be alive today.
BROWN: Teri and Patrick Caserta lost their son, Brandon, to suicide in 2018 while he was serving in the U.S. Navy, after they say he was bullied in his chain of command. They then pushed for several years to get the Brandon Act passed to allow service members to directly and confidentially seek help.
PATRICK CASERTA: When we were getting the Brandon Act passed, we went to psychologists nationwide and, in particular V.A. ones, and we talked to them. And they told us the hardest part of their job is getting somebody to the door. They said, if you get them to the door, we have a 99.9 percent success rate of helping them get back on track.
BROWN: CNN recently reported a man went overboard from the USS Abraham Lincoln earlier this month in mental distress, amid an extended deployment, renewing scrutiny on service members' well-being. In a statement posted on X, CENTCOM commander, Admiral Brad Cooper, says the Abraham Lincoln has among the lowest number of mental health cases, compared to the Navy's 11 aircraft carriers in service.
And the Navy says suicidal ideations onboard the Lincoln have gone down since the beginning.
BROWN (on camera): The military talks about the data, about suicidal ideations. Do you think people feel comfortable raising their hand to say they have it? Like, you struggled to do that? You eventually did. But it's, like, does the data really capture the reality?
HOUGH: I don't think so. I think there are so many more people struggling than we realize, because they're afraid to speak up for whatever reason it may be. Maybe it's pride. Maybe it's fear of repercussion. You know, whatever it may be. And that's sad.
BROWN (voice-over): Some of the roughly 5,000 crew members on board the USS Abraham Lincoln gave mixed reactions to CNN in July about the psychological toll from the long deployment.
BROWN (on camera): What are some of the challenges, day to day, that you have to face?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: It's kind of like "Groundhog Day."
BROWN: Yes.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: You know, reliving the same day over and over.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: It's hard. You know, it's hard on your mental state being out to sea for eight months without being on dry land.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: This is what I love to do. I love being on the deckplates.
BROWN: You do?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Yes, absolutely.
UNIDENTIFIED FEMALE: I miss my family. But being out here has been great.
CAPT. CASEY MAHON, COMMANDING OFFICER, USS DESTROYER FRANK E. PETERSEN JR.: This is the closest weapon system (?).
BROWN (voice-over): During our July visit, commanding officer, Capt. Casey Mahon, of the Destroyer Frank E. Petersen Jr., says he's well aware of the struggles of many months at sea in combat can bring.
BROWN (on camera): Every day is a big day.
MAHON: Yes.
BROWN: Potentially.
MAHON: Yes.
BROWN: How do you keep that heightened state?
MAHON: So, five and a half months on the -- on the picket line, on the firing line, is a long time. And I think it's really important to respect that challenge of that. Like (?), and be open and honest about it, right? Like it's -- it is wearing on the -- on the crew is wearing on the ship.
With that being said, I think there's two real traits that the sailors on board this ship have -- this Destroyer have.
[16:35:03]
MAHON: That sailors (INAUDIBLE) have and I think, really, sailors in general have and, if you really (?) think about it, the U.S. military, resilience and adaptability.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Stand by to recover aircraft.
BROWN (voice-over): Hough says military service, like the Navy, can be great for some and not for others like him. He eventually was airlifted off the carrier in October 2024 and medically discharged from the military that year. In the end, he says, asking for help is exactly what he needed to save his life.
HOUGH: I don't want to seem like a complainer. You know, I was there. I did my job. It wasn't what I wanted, so I tried to leave and get some help. And that worked out for me.
BROWN: Pamela Brown, CNN, Washington.
(END VIDEOTAPE)

APRIL WOODARD: Well, you know, I read an article in a magazine about the rise of the legion, and the legion being the first band to play the song from Cameo, "Talkin' Out The Side Of Your Neck."
And, you know, there was a lot of debate going on. But as I read through the article, I recalled being a child and all of the things that were being said in the article being true.
And I had a little bit more to the story too. Because, at the time, my dad was president, and I lived on the college campus. And I knew everything that was going on with the band, because I wanted to be in the band. So, --
WHITFIELD: This was in the blood.
APRIL WOODARD: This was in the blood.
And so, I told my husband about it. And he's, like, well, this has to be bigger than just Norfolk States band, because, really, the HBCU band has impacted culture worldwide.
WHITFIELD: Yes, absolutely.
APRIL WOODARD: And so, as a result of that, he then took the reins, and they did their magic.

APRIL WOODARD: Hey.
WHITFIELD: -- so I'm partial, you know, with the Bison marching band. But, I mean, this one really is like none other.
And Adrian, you all were able to get some, you know, very high-profile folks, including Sheryl Lee Ralph. We saw Kirk Franklin to also talk about and toot the horn of what the Spartan Legion is all about.
ADRIAN WOODARD II: Yes, exactly right.
WHITFIELD: How did you do that?
ADRIAN WOODARD II: There's a lot of cameos, a lot of appearances. Sheryl Lee Ralph, Timbaland, Kirk Franklin, Jay Pharoah and all those people are paying homage to the Spartan Legion, and HBCUs.
Specifically, this one is in Virginia, Norfolk. A shout out to the 757.
ADRIAN WOODARD: And I just think -- when you start thinking about the fact that people who we view as iconic also think of this school and the band as also being iconic, it does put a different perspective on it. Because we're from there, --
WHITFIELD: Yes.
ADRIAN WOODARD: -- so it's a big deal to us. But the fact that it's a big deal to them also. And super producer, you know, Timbaland looks at this band. Pharoah also looks at this band. And they make their music based upon their interaction and their impression on the band. Just it's a wonderful feeling.
APRIL WOODARD: And fun fact. Pharoah's --
WHITFIELD: Yes.
APRIL WOODARD: -- band director, when he was in high school and I think middle school, was in the Spartan Legion and was in the band. So, he was, you know, instructing Pharoah as a young man.
WHITFIELD: So, the influence is so great. It's really not just about your college experience, but it permeates beyond that. So, tell me more about your conversation, you know, mom and dad, hubby and wife, about how this came to fruition.
[16:45:03]
WHITFIELD: I mean, you had your own kind of, you know, kitchen table conversations --
ADRIAN WOODARD: Yes.
WHITFIELD: -- about, wait a minute, you know, this is really something. How do we put this magic into this documentary?
ADRIAN WOODARD: So, for us, it was personal. It's a legacy film.
WHITFIELD: Yes.
ADRIAN WOODARD: Obviously, because April's dad was president. But we met on Norfolk State's campus as teenagers. And so, I wanted -- and during our courting time and talking on the phone, I'd always hear the band in the background. It's part of our soundtrack to our -- to, you know, -- to our relationship. So, we knew this was something that we wanted to do that was special to us.
But when we begin to unfold the layers to what Norfolk State has done, who they are, and the way they have created this thing for -- not only for the region of Norfolk State, but also -- and (?) WHITFIELD: Wow.
ADRIAN WOODARD: And so, we wanted to make sure that we highlighted that. And then, of course, the greater impact of HBCUs in the culture, and what it has on the overall pop culture and mainstream. WHITFIELD: So, Adrian II, while this is in the family. You've heard
the stories, you know, and the influences all your life. It was something else to kind of peel back the layers.

ADRIAN WOODARD II: Yes, exactly. I think just the importance of music and how powerful it is. It is really something that can bring together multiple cultures, multiple generations. I use plenty of songs in the film from the 1930s all the way to today.

WHITFIELD: And it's not just about the music. I mean, just looking through the, you know, portions of your documentary. The dancing, I mean, the marching.
ADRIAN WOODARD II: Exactly right.

ADRIAN WOODARD II: Oh, yes.
WHITFIELD: It's incredible.
ADRIAN WOODARD: And, look, it's a wonderful experience for us, as a family, to be able to do this. On one hand -- and, you know, anybody who's working with your family, it's wonderful and horrible at the same time.
WHITFIELD: There can be moments, right.
ADRIAN WOODARD: Yes. Yes. Yes.
WHITFIELD: Oh, wait. And congratulations on the Emmy.
ADRIAN WOODARD II: Thank you.
ADRIAN WOODARD: Thank you so much.
ADRIAN WOODARD II: Thank you. Thank you.
WHITFIELD: That's incredible. So, how did this bring the family together even, you know, tighter?
APRIL WOODARD: I think it just brought us together because we have a different appreciation for each other. I mean, he's the cinematographer. He's the editor. He's the colorist. He -- you know, he pulled the music out.
So, I appreciate that. My husband is a visionary. He's got the branding background, the money background with J.P. Morgan. And then, I have a journalism background, so I sit down and do all of the interviews with the individuals.
And, over the years, I've maintained some pretty good relationships. And so, I'm able to get in touch with Sheryl Lee Ralph because of Scott Hamilton, my friend who was my intern back at, you know, when he was younger and I was starting in the business. So, it's --
WHITFIELD: It all comes full circle.
ADRIAN WOODARD: It does.

[16:48:37]
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
WHITFIELD: All right. Tonight, on "THE WHOLE STORY,", the rise of doomsday prepping. The number of Americans who identify themselves as preppers has doubled in less than a decade to an estimated 20 million. They include all kinds of people, regardless of gender, race, education level or geography.
But how different does doomsday prepping look for the ultra-rich? CNN's Nick Payton Walsh visited a missile silo turned luxury doomsday bunker for the super wealthy in this week's episode of "THE WHOLE STORY."
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
NICK PATON WALSH, CNN INTERNATIONAL SECURITY EDITOR (on camera): What was the cost to buy the abandoned silo itself?
LARRY HALL, SURVIVAL CONDO OWNER AND DEVELOPER: $300,000.
WALSH: But that's a bargain, right?
HALL: Oh, yes, for -- yes.
WALSH (voice-over): There is no denying the engineering feat the owner and creator, Larry Hall, has achieved here over years of detailed planning.
HALL: OK, this blue box you see is a volcanic ash scrubber. We have enough diesel fuel to run these for two and a half years. It just takes care of itself. The computers that control everything.
This is what we call the pet park. People get the feeling that, you know, they're outdoors.
I have a lot of people, ultra-high net worth individuals, that come in here. And a lot of them, when they first contact me, want to build their own private bunker. And I say, well, that's good, but why don't you come and look at what I've built?
And then, we found out that they value having other adults to bounce ideas off of, as opposed to having just their family locked in a smaller facility that they were exclusive to.
So, now you're in the bunker. Now you've got other adults saying, what do you think? Like, what's going on? How long are you --
WALSH: Even billionaires need other people.
HALL: Yes, they did. And they really appreciate that.
WALSH: So long as it's other billionaires.
HALL: Well, --
WALSH: We only have a couple of people I would put in the billionaire category.
[16:55:00]
WALSH: Other people are just successful, self-made professionals and --
(END VIDEOTAPE)
WHITFIELD: All right, Nick Payton Walsh joining me right now. Nick, great to see you.
OK. Wow, is all I've got to say. So, this former, you know, missile silo turned luxury doomsday bunker. I mean, that was quite the feat of engineering. Does he feel like he's going to have to use it?
WALSH (live): I mean, it is stunning to see that there is a part of the population, with between $1 to $5 million of spare cash, willing to get a floor in something like that. There's an elevator basically connecting about 20 floors there.
And you can get your own special space there, where you can even imagine whatever outside view you'd like on the windows. The pet park you saw there. There's even a small jail, in the event that one billionaire gets a bit too drunk in the bar they have there and picks a fight with another billionaire.
It's an extraordinary microcosm of a world there with air scrubbers, all sorts of stuff. Hydroponic gardens for lettuce. That shows you how much money you can genuinely spend, if you really do believe the world is going to end in your lifetime.
Now, I should point out, for so many Americans, the political crisis, the climate crisis, the sense of omni crisis you see on your phone the whole time, leaves that notion of this becoming much more of a mainstream fear than it was, for example, 20 or 30 years ago. Where most preppers were like, kind of, you know, white men in the woods with guns.
So, something's really changing in the climate around all of this. But the silo we saw was kind of the extreme end of expenditure there. And I kept wondering in the back of my mind, you know, this all feels so phenomenally, carefully, excellently prepared.
And it's stunning to stand in it. But surely at the end of times, there's going to be the unexpected, the unknown unknown that makes all that sort of slightly crumble, Fred.
WHITFIELD: Right. And then, I just got to wonder, just to get, you know, the most bang for their buck, so to speak, is there any -- is it being made useful in any way, in the meantime, waiting for doomsday?
WALSH: Yes. I mean, look, it's ultimately empty. And you're still seeing, kind of, Amazon packages turning up for the last time people were hanging out there.
But I think in the journey we went into, Fred, which we'll see later on, on CNN as part of "THE WHOLE STORY," it was a (?) month's long dig into this growing movement across the United States. And, you know, that's the millionaire end.
But there's still the urban survival element that we saw how to survive in the city, where many fear this is going to potentially happen.
And then, the low budget, really tough, gritty stuff that we did on a Mexican beach. Where a survival expert taught me things that, you know, you and I think we probably know. Which is how do you light a fire? How do you catch a fish? How do you build a shelter? That are actually incredibly hard and exhausting.
And to me, that was the most eye-opening part of our experience, where you're ultimately having to count the calories and the clock as you hunt for food. Knowing that the longer you do that, the less energy you have and the worst at it you're going to get. We'd got bitten live from head to toe by insects. I only ate what I could find. And, you know, if you live on coconuts, you eat more than four or five in a day, you'd definitely deteriorate, in terms of your digestive system.
WHITFIELD: And you're still not hungry. I mean, you're still not full.
WALSH: Right.
WHITFIELD: Oh, no. Did we just lose our signal? Oh, darn. OK, well, an added incentive why you must watch this evening. Nick Payton Walsh, I thank you so much.
I mean, obviously, you've got to be very industrious. Those are some of the lessons that you -- we are going to be learning by way of him to be a survivalist. Just in case you don't have a few million dollars to plunk down on a bunker.
All right. "THE WHOLE STORY WITH ANDERSON COOPER" this evening, The End of the World As We Know It, it airs tonight at 8:00 Eastern and Pacific, right here on CNN. And tomorrow on the CNN app.

Catastrophic flooding and landslides swept down China's border with Nepal earlier this week, washing away entire villages and burying others under mud. Nepali police say more than 780 people have died from the disaster. The State Department says at least 85 Americans are missing. Police also say with so many decomposing bodies and waning resources, they laid nearly 200 of the unidentified to rest in a mass grave in a forest this week. SARASWATI GHALE, FLOOD SURVIVOR, HOME DESTROYED IN MUDSLIDE (through translator): What am I feeling? Looking at everything, it's like there's no world. What is left? It's like I am dead. So, I just followed him, and he showed me the only way I could follow to survive, the only way, because if I'm going straight on, for sure, I would not be here.
