Breaking news, the first U.S. strike tonight against Iran in more than a month.

## [06] U.S. Strikes Rocket Launchers On Iran's Larak Island; Dozens Evacuated In Grand Canyon Flash Flooding; At Least 85 Americans Missing In Nepal Floods; Iran Retaliates Against U.S. Forces; GOP Grapples With Trump's Trade War, Blowback Over Beef Policies; Young Adults Use Loophole Boosting Surge in Prediction Markets; Inside the World of Doomsday Prepping

[19:00:00]
BRIAN ABEL, CNN ANCHOR: Breaking news, the first U.S. strike tonight against Iran in more than a month. Central Command says U.S. forces hit two Iranian rocket launchers on Larak Island, which is near the Strait of Hormuz. This is a critical shipping waterway for oil tankers and has become a central focus of regional tensions.
CNN's Julia Benbrook is joining us now from the White House with the very latest on this -- Julia.
JULIA BENBROOK, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Well, we do know that Iran vowed quickly to retaliate, and they are working to do that. We know that Iranian missiles were launched at Jordan. Those were intercepted according to a U.S. official. There's no impact there to report. But this comes after the -- we had learned that the -- that U.S. forces struck two Iranian launchers on Larak Island.
A U.S. official had told CNN that this happened after Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps forces were observed preparing to launch rockets with sea mines into the Strait of Hormuz. All of that coming days after President Donald Trump had said that there was a zero tolerance policy on mine placement in full force and effect, and after U.S. Central Command said that it had finished clearing the sea mines from this critical waterway.
An official tells our team, quote, "U.S. forces are monitoring the area closely and remain prepared to protect the free flow of commerce through this essential waterway. The Strait of Hormuz has, of course, been a key area of focus during this more than six-month long conflict, and there have been conflicting messages when it comes to who is in control of that strait with both sides claiming some control.
Trump, for his part, he has remained adamant that the United States is in full control of it, and he says that they are watching every inch of it.
Now, as you pointed out, Brian, these are the first strikes in this conflict in over a month. And as I noted, Iran has vowed to retaliate -- Brian. ABEL: Julia Benbrook, at the White House for us, following
developments from Iran.
Julia, thank you.
More breaking news now. In the Grand Canyon, 15 people may be missing after flash floods, according to park officials. Urgent search and rescues are now underway after heavy flooding along the park's most popular hiking trail, Bright Angel Canyon, as well as the Phantom Ranch area. Park officials say nearly all foot bridges across the Bright Angel Creek have been destroyed.
Evacuations are ongoing with dozens of people already safely evacuated from the park. The National Park Service is asking anybody who knows of hikers or backpackers who may have been in these areas to contact their tip line.
And let's bring in former FEMA external affairs officer and active first responder Jason Pack to talk about this.
Jason, when you're talking about a place like the Grand Canyon, what goes into a rescue operation like this?
JASON PACK, FORMER FEMA EXTERNAL AFFAIRS OFFICER: Danny, this is one of the most difficult terrains in the United States, really, to conduct search and rescue operations. We know the park service and the Arizona Department of Public Safety are doing those air operations. I think what's important to note is unaccounted for. We heard that term used by officials today. And that doesn't necessarily mean the worst. And at least that's certainly what we're hoping for.
That could mean that hikers don't have communication, as we've heard that the footpaths were mostly washed out. So hopefully they will be able to locate those things with aircraft, with technology like thermal imaging. These types of things to be able to find where these missing hikers are.
ABEL: Maybe splitting hairs here a little bit, Jason. But when we talk about the biggest challenges facing rescue crews, is it the terrain? Is it having to use helicopters or is it the -- just the -- how remote everything is that it might be more difficult to find people that are stranded there, especially as nightfall hits?
PACK: Absolutely. That is the challenge. In the United States, you know, unlike Nepal, we can send fire engines, ambulances, helicopters, any resource pretty much that we need is readily available to drive up there. Well, in the Grand Canyon, the problem is you can't get there except by path or by helicopter. And so even though you have all these resources available, finding those right places to use them is extremely challenging because it's measured in depth and those types of things instead of just being able to drive to the scene. And that's what makes it extremely challenging.
ABEL: (INAUDIBLE) types of treatments do people need in situations like this? PACK: Well, I think it's a good idea. That's why when you go hiking,
it's a good idea to tell someone you have to have an emergency plan and let people know where you're going. That would help in this situation. I think that's why we've seen the park service turn on their tip line for people who have that information. But they are pretty good at these search and rescue operations. They integrate well with other agencies like the Arizona Department of Public Safety.
We've seen in the past the National Guard has also come in to help.
[19:05:03]
So they have these training missions that they use to fly, and they're used to doing these types of operations. I mean, they are racing against the clock. It is going to be nightfall out there, but hopefully the hikers were prepared to and had a plan in case something like this happens. Really flash flooding is really difficult, though, because it comes so quickly and with such force. We heard in Hurricane Helene here in upper east Tennessee, we've had people that were, you know, taken from North Carolina all the way across the mountain into Tennessee with these flash floods. Hopefully that's not the case here.
ABEL: Hopefully so. Jason Pack, really appreciate your expertise on all of this. And hopefully everybody has found.
Jason, thank you.
PACK: Thanks.
ABEL: Officials in Nepal say at least 780 people have died following catastrophic floods and landslides on the China-Nepal border earlier this week. Search efforts are underway as thousands more people are still missing. Local police also say with so many decomposing bodies and waning resources, they laid nearly 200 of the unidentified dead to rest in a mass grave in a forest this week.
Now, India is sending a team of forensic scientists to help identify people's remains in Nepal. Meanwhile, families have been flying into the region from around the world to search for their loved ones. Here, you can see hundreds of missing person posters lining a hospital wall in Kathmandu. Another grim reminder of the personal toll that these catastrophic floods and landslides have taken on this region of the world. Meanwhile, survivors are returning home to find absolute devastation.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
SARASWATI GHALE, HOME DESTROYED IN MUDSLIDE (through translator): What am I feeling? Looking at everything it's like there's no world. What is left? It's like I am dead. What to do now? I don't have anything. What is there that is worth living? Now what will happen? Who knows? I am saved but how things will pan out, who knows? That's it.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABEL: At least 85 Americans are among the more than 3,000 people missing across China and Nepal. The U.S. State Department saying that nine Americans have been safely evacuated. There are no confirmed reports of U.S. citizen deaths so far.
CNN correspondent Rafael Romo has been closely watching this story, and we've been hearing from some of the families of those missing Americans.
What are you learning?
RAFAEL ROMO, CNN NATIONAL CORRESPONDENT: Yes. Just imagine the uncertainty, Brian. It happened on Wednesday. These families are still waiting to hear any news whatsoever from their loved ones. And over the last 24 hours, we've been able to get in touch with some of the families of a group of 10 men traveling together for a hiking trip of a lifetime, for both spiritual and recreational reasons.
There were nine Americans and one Canadian in the missing group of friends traveling as part of a tour as catastrophic flash flooding hit the area where they were around the Nepal-China border on Wednesday. One of those missing men is Kesava Rao Tadipatri, a 73-year-old American father who lives in Arizona. Tadipatri is the one located in the upper left corner of the following picture, holding a phone.
His daughter told CNN earlier today that her father sent his family the group photo of himself and his fellow hikers just a short time before the flood waters hit the border crossing.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
RAMYA TADIPATRI, DAUGHTER OF MISSING MAN IN NEPAL: That picture that that you showed was -- we had received at 8:36, and the tremors, the first tremors, we understand, happened at 8:37. Then at 8:42 was we received a message from my dad and he said, I will have some issues near the Trishuli River, which was that river that was flooded. And my mom asked what issues and then it was a communication blackout that was the last we heard.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: Tadipatri described her father as a spiritual guru and guide for the Hindu community, and shared with CNN that the 73-year-old had run his second marathon earlier this year.
CNN also got in touch with the wife of another member of the same group of men hiking together near the Nepal-China border when tragedy struck. Sneha Sudhir says her husband, Srinivas Sudhir Raghu, was enjoying a long anticipated hiking trip through Mount Kailash with nine other family friends.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
SNEHA SUDHIR, WIFE OF MAN MISSING IN NEPAL: He was on a hiking trip from Nepal. And then he was with nine other friends, family friends. It was supposed to be a simple two weeks' Nepal hiking trip where he would go, have some good time with his boys, and be back home.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: Sudhir also told us the hikers, including her husband, had been preparing for months for this trip, and the men, whom she described as great human beings, were all in good physical shape.
[19:10:01]
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
SUDHIR: So these boys were supposed to be going on a hike. And then that was starting from Nepal. And eventually it would be the Mount Kailash trek and be back home. That was their plan. And they had a wonderful guide. They had prepared for this particular hike for close to about three months.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: Meanwhile, help continues to arrive in the flood zone. A shipment from China just landed in Kathmandu, and the United States announced on Sunday it was sending an additional more than $3.5 million of aid. But again, we don't know how long this aid is going to last. We just saw before my story how people are coming back home to nothing whatsoever.
ABEL: And every hour, every minute that goes by without these families hearing something is just excruciating.
Rafael Romo, thank you.
And if you want to help the people in Nepal affected by this disaster, you can go to CNN.com/Impact or scan the QR code that's on your screen now.
Well, still ahead for us, more on the breaking news. We are learning of new attacks on U.S. forces by Iran, and it comes shortly after the U.S. military struck Iranian rocket launchers near the Strait of Hormuz.
The very latest coming up right here in the CNN NEWSROOM.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[19:15:57]
ABEL: Breaking news. Iran is tonight retaliating for earlier U.S. strikes on Iranian rocket launchers near the Strait of Hormuz. A U.S. official telling CNN so far Iranian missiles fired into Jordan have been intercepted.
Let's go back to the White House now, and Julia Benbrook, as well as bringing in CNN national security analyst Alex Plitsas.
And Julia, what more are you learning right now?
BENBROOK: Well, this all marks the first big escalation in this conflict in over a month as the war has dragged on for more than six months at this point. And as you pointed out, the latest here is that Iranian missiles were fired at Jordan. A U.S. official tells us that those were intercepted and that there are no significant impacts to report. That was in response to what we reported earlier that U.S. forces had struck two Iranian launchers earlier in the day.
A U.S. official told CNN that that came after Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps forces were observed preparing to launch rockets with sea mines into the Strait of Hormuz. And all of that taking place just days after President Donald Trump said that there was a no tolerance policy on mine placement in full force and effect. And after U.S. Central Command said that it had finished clearing these sea mines from that critical waterway.
An official tells CNN this. They say, "U.S. forces are monitoring the area closely and remain prepared to protect the free flow of commerce through this essential waterway." The Strait of Hormuz has been a key area of focus during this conflict, a conflict that again has gone on for more than six months now. And when those first joint U.S.-Israel strikes against Iran took place, Trump had previewed that this might only last a couple of days.
He laid out a prediction of, you know, several weeks, six weeks at some point. Now it has been more than six months. And this all is happening as poll after poll shows that Americans, the majority of them, are not happy with the way that Trump has handled this conflict at this point. So all of this escalation taking place today, we're keeping a close eye out on Trump's Truth Social.
We often hear him comment for the first time on that social media platform. He has not done so yet. He has been posting about other things, other projects, but he has not commented directly on this conflict with Iran.
ABEL: And we will wait for that comment. Julia, thank you.
Let's turn now to Alex.
And Alex, it's been a month since U.S. strikes against Iran until today. What do you believe was the tipping point for this activity tonight?
ALEX PLITSAS, CNN NATIONAL SECURITY ANALYST: So this actually started, I think about a week ago, and it began with comments from the United States, particularly from Secretary Bessent, in which he was talking about the flow of oil out of the Strait of Hormuz, and that it had increased at one point, I think, saying that it almost got to 15 million barrels last Friday, if I'm not mistaken.
And, you know, pre-war levels were about 20 to 21 million. Analysts in the industry will tell you somewhere between five to seven trending towards eight are getting out on a daily basis. Still not quite half, but getting close, certainly trending closer to U.S. official positions as to what they're saying. At the same time, the U.S. is still maintaining a military blockade of the Iranian ports, which is to add additional pressure on top of them, in addition to the sanctions package that was discussed, plus all of the destruction from the war. The Iranian economy is under pressure.
And so in response to those comments, I believe we saw a couple of things today. So earlier there was an attack on a ship off the coast of Oman, the U.K. Maritime Trade Organization. UKMTO, had put out an alert about it. So we saw an oil tanker looks like struck by a projectile. And then we saw these rocket launchers allegedly being -- you know, about to be firing some mines into the water, both of which looked like they were meant specifically to undermine the U.S. position.
So in response to that, the U.S. took offensive action to take out those launchers, as was just discussed. Now, the Iranians, as you just correctly, you know, noted as part of the program here, launched and fired on U.S. bases in Jordan. The question is, what happens next? What's been going on to date is that the U.S. has not allowed that to continue.
[19:20:01]
They've gone and gone on the offensive. The last time this happened, we saw several days of strikes along the coastal infrastructure inside of Iran to further degrade Iran's ability to attack. But again, this is another demonstration that we can't get every missile, we can't get every drone. And the Iranians are continuing to hold the Strait of Hormuz at risk to a certain extent by doing so.
ABEL: And Alex, so far, at least with the attacks aimed at Jordan, all Iranian missiles have been intercepted, the U.S. says. Will you lay out the dynamics for us here of why the aim is at Jordan right now?
PLITSAS: Sure. So there's several bases that have sort of taken the brunt of the Iranian counter-fire, and that has been in Kuwait, that has been in Jordan, that has been in the UAE, where we have all of these places. We have air bases, major air bases in the region, as well as outside of the capital city in Doha, and in Saudi Arabia, because that's where we have the majority of the air bases. So the Iranians have been firing back.
It's actually part of their four pillar strategy they have. The Iranians are, you know, for all the comments that are made about them, they are a rational actor. They do know that they understand that they are at military overmatch against the United States. And so they're pursuing a four part strategy. The first is to try to maximize casualties on the U.S. side, which, again, why we saw these attacks on U.S. bases today.
It's to increase the cost of goods and flow of commerce by choking off the Strait of Hormuz. It is an attempt to undermine the president's base at home, going up and leading into the elections while they're there, and then to put pressure on the Gulf states and the Arab states by firing at them to try to get them to pressure the United States into ending the war. The latter part has failed.
So this tonight was an attempt to strike U.S. forces there. Glad that everything was intercepted. But let's be honest, these were missiles that were fired at U.S. military bases with the intention of killing U.S. service members had they struck their targets. This was an attempted lethal attack against the United States military and forces in the region. So a serious action. But the president has got two tracks on this.
Let's not forget, you know, when we assassinated Qassem Soleimani and they responded with ballistic missile attacks five days later against our bases in Iraq, there were traumatic brain injuries that were reported afterwards, but there were no physical injuries or deaths. The president chose not to respond to that. In recent weeks, he has chosen to respond to attacks on U.S. bases, sort of sending a message to the Iranians.
So the president can go either way on this. He can say, hey, nobody was hurt or killed. We shot everything down. Let's move on. Or we could see several days of strikes afterwards. So I think if we see additional strikes, they could be substantive again, like we saw the last time. So it's going to be a question of whether or not the U.S. wants to reengage or if they're going to continue to let this economic blockade that they've instituted really take hold and see how much pressure the Iranians will take before they come back to the table.
ABEL: All right. We shall see. Alex Plitsas and Julia Benbrook, thank you both.
Well, Americans, especially those in large agriculture areas, they are dealing with a triple tariff hit. After the break, just how big of an impact President Trump's moves are having on GOP leaders in those areas.
You're in the CNN NEWSROOM.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[19:27:16]
ABEL: President Trump is facing blowback from cattle ranchers and rural Republicans over decision to pause tariffs on some foreign beef imports. Last hour, I spoke with John Boyd, Jr., president of the National Black Farmers Association. He says this move is not putting America's farmers first.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
JOHN BOYD, JR., PRESIDENT AND FOUNDER, NATIONAL BLACK FARMERS ASSOCIATION: We are at a crossroads. And, you know, this election season is coming up in midterm elections. And I hope farmers will vote their pocketbook and their situations that we're facing right now. We are facing the lowest cattle count here in the United States since 1951. So we're not producing a lot of beef here. And the reason is, is because we're not investing real resources into America's farmers.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABEL: All right. Let's talk about this now. We're joined by CNN political commentators David Urban and Karen Finney. David, a Republican strategist and former Trump campaign adviser. Karen, a senior adviser to the 2016 Clinton presidential campaign and a former DNC communications director.
David, first to you. It's a triple hit of tariffs, high gas and fertilizer prices. Now imported beef. How much does this weigh down Republicans in agriculture heavy states?
DAVID URBAN, CNN SENIOR POLITICAL COMMENTATOR: Brian, you know, it's pretty tough in those states up in the northern, you know, northern border, North Dakota, Kansas, lots of other places, where cattle production is key. And, you know, as you point out, the fertilizer prices have an impact as well because -- and so do gas prices because you need to grow the food, the cows and cattle eat, right?
So it's kind of a vicious circle. I do believe it is, you know, it's hurting. I do -- I think the administration is looking to try to move to ameliorate some of that with perhaps country of origin labeling on some of this beef. So to give consumers a choice, allow people to try to drive down cost of meat while giving them -- you know, allowing American farmers to compete. If you want to buy all American beef, you can buy it.
But giving other options to drive down some of the costs. But, you know, you -- you're the president, you're stuck with all the things that go wrong in the economy, whether you cause them or not at this point.
ABEL: Well, say, how much of it is his own making right now with this particular situation?
URBAN: Yes. Well, the tariffs -- listen, the tariffs are -- well, the Middle East obviously, the situation in the Straits of Hormuz obviously has driven up gas prices, although were returning to somewhat normal there. Same with fertilizer. But that's a lagging. It's not going to be normal until sometime well after the election, right? So even if things were to reopen tomorrow, gas prices still remain high. Fertilizer prices high, all those things high.
This move on beef is somewhat curious, right? I'm not quite sure why you add insult to injury at this point. I think, you know, a lot of elected officials down ballot, right, congressmen and senators in those states, kind of scratching their head and asking the administration like, why? I think there is a lot of frustration amongst those rank-and-file members in the House and Senate.
[19:30:19]
ABEL: Karen, quite curious is one way to put it. What do Democrats -- what are the message need to be in response? Is it enough to just be anti-Trump in this moment, or do they need to be laying out a plan for what they could actually do if given majorities in the House, and maybe even the Senate?
KAREN FINNEY, CNN POLITICAL COMMENTATOR: Yes, Look, as David knows well, it is never good enough to just be anti your opponent. You ve got to also talk to voters about what you're for and that is part of why you're hearing, I think our voters -- I am really proud of our candidates. They are laser focused in talking about the issues that are impacting people.
You know, in the Midwest, as we were talking about, it is fertilizer costs. It is -- you know, that direct hit to ranchers and farmers, it is gas prices -- I mean, gas prices is one that is obviously meaningful across the country. I was just in South Carolina for a week, and you could tell people are really hurting.
But also, you know, the thing that I think our candidates are doing well is also talking about costs and affordability in the language of what people are dealing with in their states.
Health care is another big one. But you ve got someone like Mary Peltola who is talking about fish because fishing is a big deal in Alaska and that's a different conversation than you might be having in the Midwest versus the South.
Although the underlying issues are similar and I will just say, you know, listening to the segment just before us, when people see the sort of chaos in the Middle East, that does not assuage their concerns about whether or not prices are going to come down, and then when they see the President taking to social media to attack journalists, to talk about building his art, or all of these other things, what it says to them, what it reinforces is he is focused on those things. He is not focused on me. He is not focused on my concerns.
So I think you're going to continue to see our candidates focusing on the everyday concerns people have, but we still have to talk about the fact that Trump had a golden opportunity to do a lot for this country, and instead, he is been focusing on his own grift and his own ability to make now billions of dollars for himself and his family.
ABEL: And David, I want to talk about some of those concerns that Karen listed there. CNN's Adam Cancryn and Kristen Holmes had reporting just last week about concerns over the upcoming Republican Midterm Convention, dubbed Trumpapalooza, saying, "Republicans are privately questioning if it strikes the right tone amid the electorate's tightening budgets and the wisdom of diverting candidates from their districts just weeks out from Election Day," at a time when people are concerned with the cost of living.
So is it a good look? Is it a smart strategy to be hosting an event like this, David?
URBAN: Yes, so here is what I'd say to that. I am not super concerned. I think that Karen knows to run lots of campaigns as well. These midterm elections are about voter turnout, turning out the base. President Trump alluded to this.
He did an interview last week in The White House where he said, look, you know, people are saying that he is blaming House members. Well, you know, quite frankly, the electorate is pretty upset with the Congress writ large for a lot of things that they are doing. Trump is on the ballot.
And so the President is going to get out there. He is going to try to motivate, you know, the electorate. The Republican base electorate feels that Congress hasn t done enough to support the President's agenda that's why they are upset. So the President is going to get out there, try to motivate those base voters.
Look, he may be unpopular with the electorate writ large, but amongst Republicans, he is still in the high -- mid to high 80s and so these elections are about turning out your base.
If we can turn out base Republican voters in these elections, they are all very close. These are going to be all very close elections. If we can do that, Republicans will hang tough. I don t think we will lose the senate. The House is a different story. It is all about, you know, stemming losses there.
And I think -- I think he is doing the right thing. If you're going to divert House members away from campaigning in their districts, asking them to come there, that may not be smart, but Donald Trump getting out and rallying and going to some of these districts, it depends, too, Brian what districts he is going to and where is he going to try to target.
But, you know, I think Trump is on the ballot, whether you like it or not in the district. And so he might as well be out front trying to whip up voters to come out and vote.
ABEL: And Karen, the last word to you.
FINNEY: All right, I would just point out that in 2022, we had something historic happen and that was Joe Biden didn t lose in the midterms the way we historically see. Why? Because people agreed at the time, and we had problems then with what he was doing.
So if people believe that what Trump was doing and that what Republicans were doing was right for the country and benefiting them, you wouldn t necessarily see what looks like could be a very historic wave. Obviously, we have real concerns about making sure that everyone gets to vote.
[19:35:10]
The President is going to continue to create chaos when it comes to actually voting, but the trends that are moving in our direction are also because people are angry at Republicans for not holding Trump accountable to the things that he promised. So we will see what happens in November.
I think we are going to have a few more twists and turns between now and then.
ABEL: Just a couple of months away.
URBAN: No, no. No drama. No drama, Brian.
ABEL: None at all.
FINNEY: No!
ABEL: We will have to leave it there. David Urban and Karen Finney, thank you guys, as always.
The young people are finding a loophole for online betting and it has lawmakers and addiction experts concerned.
CNN's Marshall Cohen digs into the multibillion dollar industry after the break. You're in the CNN NEWSROOM.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[19:40:31]
ABEL: A Division 2 college football player in Missouri has died after suffering a medical emergency during the first quarter of his team's season opener.
William Jewell College running back, MicahJo Barnett scored a touchdown shortly before the medical emergency and was taken to a local hospital, where he later died.
The school suspended the game and held a candlelight vigil last night to honor him. No other details about his death have been released yet.
Trying to find Lake Ontario on Google Maps.
Well, if you are in the United States, you won't. As of this weekend, users in the U.S. will now see the lake identified as Lake America.
The change comes after an executive order from President Trump amid his tense trade war with Canada. He, however, can't force other countries to follow suit.
And young adults are betting big on the prediction market. According to new CNN analysis, people aged 18 to 21 have traded more than $5 billion on the Kalshi prediction market, and that's raising the concern of addiction experts and lawmakers, who warn they could also be gambling on their financial future.
CNN senior reporter, Marshall Cohen has the story.
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
MARSHALL COHEN, CNN SENIOR REPORTER: $5 billion, that is the estimate for the trading volume on Kalshi from users 18, 19 and 20. And I'll tell you why that is such an important cohort. It's because most people don't realize that prediction markets aren't treated as gambling under the law as it stands right now. They're treated more like the stock market and other financial markets that are 18-plus, while sports books and casinos in most states are 21-plus.
Critics call that a loophole. Public health experts have spoken out about this and said that they believe it puts younger adults at risk for addiction and to take risk of your behaviors that could lose them their money.
So, here are the numbers. $5.4 billion in estimated trading volume this year, so far on Kalshi for these 18 to 21 users. And $4 billion of that is from sports and parlays, which are incredibly popular with sports.
And everyone I spoke to has said that is going to explode in the coming weeks and months because the NFL season is right around the corner.
So for its part, Kalshi insists that it takes this very seriously and that it is always looking out for its younger users. They say that they have safeguards, that they impose voluntarily on the platform to make sure that those traders are protected. Things like deposit limits and warnings on the app if the system identifies a problematic trading patterns.
Kalshi also donated $2 million to a group that combats addiction on gambling and trading platforms.
However, the states just aren't buying it. Look at this map. Forty- four states across the country have banded together to push for more regulation in these prediction markets.
Like I said, they're currently regulated by the federal government and that means they're 18-plus. The states want this to be treated like gambling, like casinos, subject to state laws, state taxes and that would be 21-plus.
And these are blue states, red states, battleground states. It really spans the entire political landscape.
By the way, CNN has a partnership with Kalshi. We use its data to cover some major events, but editorial employees like myself and others are not allowed to use prediction sites.
Back to you.
(END VIDEOTAPE)
ABEL: Marshall Cohen, thank you.
Well, apparently many Americans think the end of the world as we know it could be near and are stocking up to be ready just in case of a doomsday scenario.
After the break, we talk to Nick Paton Walsh about his inside look at how survivalists live.
You're in the CNN NEWSROOM.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[19:49:29]
ABEL: Get this, one-third of Americans have spent some part of their household budget on survival equipment or supplies, like an end of the world scenario.
And an estimated $20 million Americans now self-identify as preppers, a number that has doubled since 2017. Up next, in this week's all new episode of "The Whole Story," CNN' Nick Paton Walsh dives into the prepping world with a popular YouTuber and urban survival expert.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
NICK PATON WALSH, CNN CHIEF INTERNATIONAL SECURITY CORRESPONDENT (voice over): Jack shows us his safe house, where his training gets more violent and the gear gets serious. AR-15, Sigs, night vision, this one with a thermal clip on adapter.
[19:50:09]
JACK, SURVIVALIST: If you come at me with a number one strike, go ahead. Give me the number one. I am going to cut your arm, so go ahead and give it to me again. So then I am going to step out. I am going to trap the arm.
All right. So, you are going to slap. So follow my hand this way. Do not you do not have to grab it. Just slap it. Cut so you are going to know you are going to follow me with this hand. One, cut control the arm. There you go.
WALSH: Would you mind slowing down your attacks?
There you go. And then that. Right. Okay.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: So then I can cut.
WALSH: Right. Yes Fine. Okay. I am very dead.
Was that wrong?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: No. That's good. Yeah. That's good.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
[19:50:56]
ABEL: CNN chief national security correspondent, Nick Paton Walsh is joining us now. Nick, tell us what more you learned.
WALSH: You know, an extraordinary month's long dig into this world of prepping, as you said, something increasingly mainstream now.
And while someone like Jack, you saw their teaching skills that kind of hark towards the stereotype there, Jack a bit right-wing on the spectrum, you might argue, he says, his clientele has changed massively in the past years.
He is now seeing tech people, entrepreneurs coming to him to learn things they hope they never have to actually employ, including that knife fighting there, a big tip from Jack, never get in a knife fight. Run if you can.
But if you have an almost limitless budget, ultimately there is no end of stuff. You could spend your money on. Billionaires, millionaires, the ultra-rich spending huge amounts of cash, $5 million per apartment, potentially in tens of thousands each year on upkeep fees for the possibility that they have the most luxurious way of spending the end of times.
I should point out, it is still pretty unlikely, despite the political crisis, the climate crisis, the sense of endless omni crisis you get piped at you during your phone that we are going to live through some kind of massively apocalyptic event, you might argue, but still, people spending huge amounts of money on that eventuality.
Here is one extraordinary engineering feat we got to see inside.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
WALSH: What was the cost to buy the abandoned silo itself?
LARRY HALL, OWNER & DEVELOPER, SURVIVAL CONDO: Three hundred thousand.
WALSH: But that's a bargain, right?
HALL: Oh, yes. Certain, yes.
WALSH: There is no denying the engineering feat owner and creator Larry Hall has achieved here over years of detailed planning.
HALL: Okay, this blue box you see is a volcanic ash scrubber. We have enough diesel fuel to run these for two-and-a-half years. It just takes care of itself. The computers that control everything.
This is what we call the pet park. People get the feeling that, you know, they're outdoors.
I have a lot of people, ultra-high net worth individuals that come in here. And a lot of them, when they first contact me, want to build their own private bunker. And I say, well, that's good. But why don't you come and look at what I've built? And then we found out that they value having other adults to bounce ideas off of, as opposed to having just their family locked in a smaller facility that they were exclusive to.
So now you're in a bunker. Now you've got other adults. Say, what do you think? Like, what's going on?
WALSH: So, you and billionaires need other people?
HALL: Yes, they did. And they really appreciated that.
WALSH: So long as it's other billionaires.
HALL: No, we only have a couple of people I would put in the billionaire category. Other people are just successful self-made professionals.
(END VIDEO CLIP) ABEL: And Nick, just fascinating stuff. Was there anything that you learned that surprised you when researching for this episode?
WALSH: Yes, I mean, we spent a large part of this. It felt like a very long time on a sliver of Mexican beach in the middle of nowhere, an old ruined fishing hut learning the most serious and basic of survival skills, and I ultimately learned I am completely ill equipped for something like that.
But the key thing there was if you break it all down to its most basic, what do you need to survive every day if you have nothing in the wild? There is an awful lot of work and energy you have to apply to the most basic things like how to fish potentially when you have nothing. How many coconuts can you eat a day without getting diarrhea? How do you make a shelter? How do you get fresh water?
How, most importantly, do you make fire, which you can purify water with, cook food with? I was useless at that. Even the most basic things in all that left me utterly bewildered and stranded. We had a phenomenal guide. Amos Rodriguez, a survival expert himself, who kind of kept me alive through all of this, but got bitten from head to toe horizontal rain, getting into even the most advanced of hammocks that I brought with me.
And that kind of taught you really how far we've come from those initial survival skills that maybe our great, great grandparents never thought twice about that ultimately now here, when most of the fish I get is delivered on a polystyrene tray in a supermarket, how hard it is to look back towards those things that hopefully will never need one day.
[19:55:10]
An interesting journey for us to go through. This total panoply from surviving in the city to surviving on a beach, to trying to do this with millions, to potentially surviving on so much less indeed, extraordinary to see how this thought process is becoming so much more part of mainstream America -- Brian.
ABEL: Yes, certainly an eye opening experience and journey.
Nick Paton Walsh, Nick, thank you.
And be sure to catch it, "The Whole Story" with Anderson Cooper, "The End of the World as We Know It" next right here on CNN.
Thank you for joining me. I am Brian Abel. Have a good night.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[20:00:48]

## [17] Rescuers Race To Save Survivors After Catastrophic China-Nepal Floor; War With Iran At A Stalemate Six Months In; Canada Pushes Back On Trump's Effort To Rename Lake Ontario

[00:00:00]
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
BRIAN ABEL, CNN ANCHOR: Hello and welcome to our viewers joining us around the world. I'm Brian Abel. Here's what's ahead. The rush to find survivors in Nepal grows more desperate by the hour, as the window to rescue them is rapidly closing. And President Trump said the war with Iran would only last a few weeks. Now six months later, it's at a stalemate. We'll take a look at what may come next as this conflict drags on.
Plus, troubled waters. Canadians are pushing back after Trump makes waves by trying to rename Lake Ontario as Lake America.
ANNOUNCER: Live from Atlanta, this is CNN Newsroom with Brian Abel.
ABEL: Rescue teams are racing to find survivors following catastrophic flash floods along Nepal's border with China. Right now, an urgent search is underway for workers who may be trapped in hydropower tunnels. Crews from India, China, and South Korea are now joining the response, bringing critical expertise and resources to strengthen relief efforts.
Nepal's army says it's deployed more than 8,000 service members to help. About 3,000 people are still missing, but a sweeping rescue effort has saved the lives of nearly 3,000 more. Dramatic new video shows one of those rescues. A young girl trapped under a collapsed building.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
Video is shown of a young girl being rescued from a collapsed building in Nepal.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABEL: Crews took her to a camp that evening. She is now receiving treatment. Authorities say she told rescuers her name is Manisha (ph), and she's six years old. The death toll does continue to climb, and authorities are struggling to cope with the mounting number of bodies. More than 734 people are now confirmed dead in Nepal, and at least 16 others in China. Meanwhile, officials are warning that water levels have surged in a
river near the flood zone, raising fears of further flooding. They sent a text to residents asking them to be alert, but did not ask them to evacuate. CNN's Hanako Montgomery has more on the heartbreaking search for the flood's victims.
HANAKO MONTGOMERY, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Here in Nepal's Devighat, destruction stretches for miles. Daily life has grounded to a halt here, replaced instead by a steady stream of police and dogs sniffing for any bodies. And actually, just moments ago, they did find one. We of course can't show it to you, but they told us that because it was so badly decomposed, they couldn't even identify who it was.
Downstream, workers are digging a mass grave for the hundreds of bodies that have washed ashore, delivered by the truck load. Some are so heavily waterlogged, workers struggle under their weight. And among these bodies is this five-year-old girl. Serena's (ph) family said she was on her way to school when the flood swept her bus away. We spoke to her father, Rajkumar (ph), by phone. He barely survived and is now stranded with help out of reach.
When did you learn, sir, that your daughter was missing.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE (on the phone) (through translator): I was working, and I called immediately after I escaped. I was still hoping that my daughter was alive. One of the girls who tried saving her told me she wasn't able to hold her, and the flood swept her away.
MONTGOMERY: I can't imagine the -- the pain you must be feeling right now. How are you dealing with this immense tragedy?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: My dream was always to educate my daughter, so she could be successful in life. That's why I worked so hard. But my dream for her hasn't come true.
[00:05:00]
MONTGOMERY: But Rajkumar says he can't identify her body, with no money or means to confirm that his only child is now gone. Numbers can't describe the human cost of this tragedy, a pain shared by too many here. Sadip Nepal (ph) lost his business in the flood and his home, the one he's lived in for years.
How long do you think it's going to take for you to rebuild all of this?
SADIP NEPAL: SURVIVED FLOOD: I think it is not possible to stay here. I think we have to leave this place.
MONTGOMERY: Sadip barely made it out alive when he saw the flood rushing toward his town. He said, like a tsunami. He ran up a nearby mountain where he filmed these videos. He said all he had was a T- shirt and sandals, but luckily his whole family made it out in time.
He shows us inside his home, where furniture and a bathroom once stood, are now tree branches, ropes, and animal carcasses that the river brought in. Now more than three days after the flood swept through the valley, the chances of finding trapped survivors alive are getting slimmer.
Many of these operations are now becoming search and recovery, a mounting toll of heartbreak for thousands of families. Hanako Montgomery, CNN, Nepal.
ABEL: And joining me now is CNN's Kristie Lu Stout in Hong Kong. And Kristie Lu, those -- those images there that we just saw from Hanako, not only heart-breaking, but just really showing how difficult the rescue efforts are.
KRISTIE LU STOUT, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Absolutely, Brian. And Brian, there is immense devastation and loss. Search operations have resumed in the disaster zone, and authorities are struggling. They are struggling to reach remote areas, including tunnels under hydroelectric projects where scores of workers remain missing.
They are struggling through very difficult conditions like rain and fog, and they are so struggling with the sheer scale of it all. At least 750 people are dead. 3,000 people are missing. Among the missing, 900 hydroelectric workers. And right now, we know that rescuers are urgently pumping air into a tunnel under a hydropower plant where those workers are missing, and the hope here, the plan here, is to drill a manhole to send rescue teams there to look for the missing and to find any survivors.
Late on Saturday night, we heard a statement from a Nepalese official. According to Nepal's Minister of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation, let's bring up the statement for you, saying this quote: "There has been some success in the ongoing rescue efforts at the Trishuli-3A Hydropower Project Tunnel, which is buried under mud and sludge following the flood.
Now, look, that's very encouraging news. There are reports of some progress, but this official notes that they have yet to establish any communication with the trapped workers in those tunnels. Now the families of the missing they are trapped in this agonizing wait for any updates about their loved ones. Survivors are waiting for aid to arrive as the roads remain impassable, and some survivors have been able to return home to see what is left to try to retrieve any belongings.
But for a vast majority of them, there is nothing left. I want you to listen to the testimony of this 43-year-old villager. Watch this.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
SARASWATI GHALE, 43-YEAR-OLD VILLAGER (through translator): What am I feeling? Looking at everything, it's like there is no world. What is left? It's like I am dead. What to do now? I don't have anything. What is there that is worth living? Now, what will happen? Who knows? I am saved, but how things will pan out, who knows? That's it.
LU STOUT: Now, for the survivors, the survivors face a daunting task of rebuilding their lives, and for Nepal, an already impoverished country, facing the very daunting task of rebuilding vast parts of a nation.
According to an interview with Reuters, we heard from the financial minister of Nepal saying that the country needs between $4 billion to $5 billion to rebuild. It is urgently requesting foreign aid to rebuild lost transport links like bridges, communication lines. It urgently needs aid for tunnel experts to come in to help locate the missing, as well as technical expertise in the identification of remains, the storage of remains, and DNA analysis.
We know that the United States has pledged $3.6 million worth of aid, but for Nepal, already one of the world's most poorest nations, that amount is just a fraction of what it desperately needs right now. Brian?
ABEL: Yeah, they will certainly need the entire world to come help them with that daunting task of rebuilding.
LU STOUT: Yes.
ABEL: CNN's Kristie Lu Stout for us in Hong Kong. Kristie, thank you.
A group of hikers from Virginia are among the thousands missing after the disaster. The wife of one of the 10 missing men is pleading for help in finding them.
[00:10:00]

So, it's going to be coming soon.
Just -- just not us, or these guys are not in the place where all this thing is happening. So that -- that was my initial thought, and but then yes, looks like I -- I don't know where they are. It's a family that's out there. It's 10 boys who are stuck there. Everyone is a great human being, a great dad, a great husband, and I'm talking on behalf of the entire family of 10 that I'm representing here.
We want to seek help from public, government, military. I don't know who's going to help me, but if you guys can start looking for them, because there are some of them who needs their medical attention on a day-to-day basis. I also have one of my elderly brother who had joined there, and he is -- he is 70 plus, super fit. Many of them run their marathons, but of course, see they're not ready for something like this.
And as I said, yes, they need their medications on a timely basis. They of course need to take their food. I don't know what not, but 80 hours is way too long for them to be somewhere where they are not taken care of.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABEL: We do want to show you some striking before and after satellite images of Nepal that truly speak to the scale of this disaster. You can clearly see the heavily swollen rivers tracking through the steep mountain valleys of the region after bursting their banks. Whole villages and road networks have been wiped off the map.
Many of those villages are remote and difficult to access under the best conditions. Now some are completely cut off, and that really puts into perspective just how challenging search and rescue efforts are in a disaster of this scale, and those already challenging search and rescue efforts will likely be complicated even more by rainfall.
The region is in the middle of its monsoon season with thunderstorms possible across the flood zone over the next couple of days. CNN meteorologist Alison Chinchar has latest forecast.
ALISON CHINCHAR, CNN METEOROLOGIST: The last thing this area needs is the potential for additional flooding. Unfortunately, there are rain showers expected in the forecast over the next several days. You can see through Sunday afternoon and Sunday evening, we get another round of showers that makes its way through. Same thing for Monday and even into Tuesday.
Both of those days are likely going to have breaks in the morning before we start to see activity pick back up in the afternoon and evening hours, which is really kind of the main timeline for this general area because we are right in the middle of monsoon season. August is the second wettest month of the year, and for many of these areas, they'll likely pick up 50 to 100 millimeters a day over the next few days.
That's roughly two to four inches. So again, you're talking about a lot of rain, and a lot of that starts to build back up, bringing more rain to a lot of those river valleys that you've seen in many of the images and videos. The big concern too is also where a lot of the mud flows have occurred. That area, when it hardens, turns to an almost concrete-like material.
But once you add water back into the mix from rain showers, it liquefies, making it very unstable and very dangerous for a lot of the rescue and the recovery crews to come in. Not to mention, it just continues to cause more messes and potentially increase the likelihood of additional flooding.
ABEL: Alison Chinchar, thank you. Far away from the tragedy in Nepal, Nepali communities around the world are praying for the dead and the missing. In Sydney, Australia, for example, more than 100 people gathered for a candlelight vigil. One man whose friend is missing says the losses have really hit home.

NAGENDRA ADHIKARI, HUMANITARIAN WORKER: We are sad for his family. We're still hoping for the good news. Not only that, some of my cousin, some of my relatives, their beloved are also missing. It's a huge disaster, and we are facing it.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABEL: And in London, a worship ceremony for a Hindu festival took a somber tone because of the devastating floods, but it also doubled as a fundraiser since many attendees have ties to the region. Organizers say they raised more than $8,000 for -- for relief efforts and hope to collect more.

NARENDRA KANDEL, CHAIR OF COMMITTEE AT SHREE PASHUPATINATH TEMPLE: This is the temple, you know, where we always come together. Difficult time, and people talk. You know, they try to support each other, and I mean emotionally and at the same time financially as well. You know, and people are being very generous you know, to support Nepal.
(END VIDEO CLIP)

In the six months since the U.S. and Israel launched the war with Iran, the financial and economic impacts of the conflict have reverberated across the world, but none of the original goals laid out by President Donald Trump or Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu have been achieved.
CNN Jerusalem Bureau Chief Oren Liebermann has more.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
OREN LIEBERMANN, CNN JERUSALEM BUREAU CHIEF: It was six months ago that the U.S. and Israel launched the opening strikes of the war against Iran, and now, as we sit here half a year later, that war very much ongoing and unresolved. President Donald Trump and Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu laid out what seemed to be clear goals for the war: destroy Iran's nuclear program, end its ballistic missile program, and end its support for regional proxies.
Now, however, with none of that accomplished, the Trump Administration has shifted to a different goal and a different strategy. Iran used the war and learned throughout the war that they really didn't need a military able to compete with the U.S. or Israel to hold hostage the Strait of Hormuz and close down one of the world's most critical waterways through which 20 percent of the world's oil flows.
That became Iran's most powerful weapon, and that is what the U.S. is trying to open right now, so that there aren't a global ripple effect of impacts from the closed or limited Strait of Hormuz, and of course, so that there aren't incredibly high gas prices in the U.S. ahead of the midterms. That is the only goal, it seems, that the U.S. is focusing on right now, trying to make sure the Strait of Hormuz is open and free for traffic. It is limited here.
The Trump Administration is trying to boast that it's open for traffic, and yet the traffic through the Strait that it's talking about has had to travel through the Strait with its transponder off and with some level of U.S. Navy escort or monitoring to make that happen, and that's because even after the U.S. and Israel destroyed much of Iran's navy and air force and military, they're still able to leverage that power on the Strait of Hormuz with drones, with short- range rockets, and frankly, shipping companies and insurers aren't willing to take that risk.
And so, we're in a state now where Iran has shown that it has ability to exert pressure on the Strait of Hormuz without a final resolution here. Meanwhile, the Trump Administration has said in recent days that there are no negotiations with Iran. That even as Qatar is trying to negotiate with Iran and Oman to try to find some sort of resolution on the Strait itself, and that remains the challenge here: finding a solution that both satisfies the U.S. and Iran.
Despite President Donald Trump repeatedly claiming that Iran is begging for a deal, Iran has repeatedly shown that even with the economic pressure they're facing, the inflation, and all of the other problems, they're not begging for a deal, and they're willing to make very high demands to open up the Strait for the world's oil to flow through it. Oren Liebermann, CNN in Jerusalem.
(END VIDEO CLIP) ABEL: Oren, thank you. There is much more to come here on CNN

But the very real risk at this moment is the continued rain and the development of this second lake, which is being monitored. identifying children who may be unaccompanied or separated from their families. Now, this is -- this is incredibly likely. They were not close to their parents.
And so, we've met with children who were separated and now, thankfully, have been reunited. ABEL: Canada is pushing back against President Trump's attempt to rename one of the Great Lakes along the New York-Ontario border on Saturday. Ontario Premier Doug Ford unveiled a large waterfront sign that reads "Lake Ontario Now and Always." It comes in response to President Trump's executive order to change the body of water to Lake America on Thursday after trade talks between the two nations collapsed.
The name Ontario is derived from indigenous roots, which predate the establishment of both Canada and the U.S.. Ford says it will remain unchanged no matter what President Trump wants to call it.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
DOUG FORD, ONTARIO PREMIER: So that's -- that's our message to President Trump. We're resilient, and we're strong Canadians, and we're always going to protect our sovereignty, always. And we'll -
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABEL: All right. For more on this, let's bring in Diamond Isinger live from Vancouver, a Policy Strategist and former Special Advisor on Canada-U.S. relations to Prime Minister Justin Trudeau. Thank you for your time, Diamond. A professor from my alma mater, Western Michigan University, when writing a Washington Post op-ed this week about the name change, leaned in on this little-known poet named Shakespeare, remembering Juliet's question to Romeo: "What's in a name? That which we call a rose by any other word would smell as sweet."
The contemplation highlighting that the act of changing the label doesn't actually change the nature of the thing, and then us Michiganders still call the concert venue that got a corporate name change Pine Knob. Does this proclamation by President Trump, renaming Lake Ontario hold any water on the other side of the border or any weight for the future of the lake?
DIAMOND ISINGER, FMR. SPECIAL ADVISOR ON CANADA-U.S. RELATIONS TO JUSTIN TRUDEAU: This week's proclamation by the president, I think, was just the latest in a line-up of comments from him and his administration that have caused further challenge in our Canada-U.S. relationship. We're just over a week past now the essentially collapse of talks between Canada and the United States to try to get to a trade agreement around potentially reducing or eliminating tariffs being applied to Canada.
And this latest offense towards Canada is only galvanizing Canadians to be even more frustrated and even less inclined to want to negotiate with the Trump Administration, despite the significant challenge that Canadians are facing during this trade war.
ABEL: So, that opposite effect. Some of our audience might actually know that the two countries signed an agreement over this lake and the other Great Lakes back in the 70s, working together on the Great Lakes Water Quality Agreement, that's since become kind of a neglected funding stream.
But it's just one of a plethora of examples of the two countries' cooperation as close allies. Do you see a future where that cooperation, that kind, becomes cohesive once again, or -- or is this strong enough of situation to cause permanent damage?
ISINGER: The Canada-U.S. relationship is far more significant than just trade, though trade is obviously a dominant theme. We have lots of economic opportunity between our two countries, many jobs in both countries that rely on this continued trade. But as you allude to, we also have opportunities for environmental cooperation. We share a massive shared border between our two countries.
Those have long been opportunities for Canada and the U.S. to work quite closely together. But all of that is challenged and jeopardized by this trade situation, and it's not clear when we can get to a better place, other than perhaps at the conclusion of President Trump's tenure as President of the United States.
ABEL: OK, let's talk about the real-time impacts of this fracture now, Diamond. It's certainly having an impact on trade, on people's livelihoods. You were an advisor to Justin Trudeau. If you were an advisor right now to Mark Carney, what would you be telling him?
ISINGER: I think he made exactly the right decision at this time last week of walking away from the table and ceasing those at that time conversations with the U.S. administration. They were made further complicated by, as has been widely reported, both the role of the U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer, but then also having Secretary Lutnick enter the mix as another interlocutor.
There were 11th-hour requests and demands made of Canada that were a surprise, contrary to what had been discussed to date between Canada and the United States. That eventually got things to a point where Canada did not feel that it could reach a good deal that was indeed beneficial for Canada, but also mutually beneficial for the two countries.
[00:40:00]
So, I actually think the decisions that the Prime Minister has made at this time last week and since have been correct. But now we find ourselves in this position of unfortunately heading towards this retaliatory tariff imposition date of September 8th, when Canada will have a new suite of tariffs imposed against U.S. products as our response to these unfortunate unilateral actions that the president has taken.
ABEL: Do Canadians agree with you that Prime Minister Carney is doing what he should be doing, or are they asking for more to be done, more retaliation for President Trump's actions?
ISINGER: Well, just like public opinion reported on your network, we've had lots of opinion solicited in the last week here domestically in Canada. By some counts, at least three quarters of Canadians believe that we should be retaliating against the United States right now, and not against everyday Americans, but against the administration's decisions.
It's so unfortunate because this trade war obviously has real life consequences for Canadians, but it has real life consequences for Americans, and we don't need to find ourselves in this situation. To your question, though, whether there should be more done, I think we're going to have to see how the coming days and weeks unfold, whether or not the United States explores any additional new and novel experimental measures to introduce new tariffs, for example, as has been done over recent months.
And notably, the U.S. administration has sort of shown its hand in revealing what its vulnerabilities are with Canada because it has not imposed its upcoming 50 percent -- or rather current 50 percent tariffs on things like our natural resource exports, the U.S. is incredibly reliant on. So many in Canada are still talking about and exploring whether or not it makes sense to add those into our retaliatory strategy, something like an export tariff on those products that we supply as key inputs for U.S. manufacturing and households across the U.S.
ABEL: All right, Diamond Isinger in Vancouver very much appreciate your expertise. Thank you for joining us.
ISINGER: Thank you.

UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Egypt.
YEUNG: Egypt.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Afghanistan.
YEUNG: Afghanistan.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Iraq.
YEUNG: Iraq.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Jordan.
YEUNG: Jordan. So, all different countries. How are you all doing here?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Doing here, like hell.
YEUNG: Like hell?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Yeah. No internet, no electric, too much of mosquito. Everybody's had malaria.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE (through translator): The police detained us and then demanded money from us. Since that day, we have not left the building. YEUNG: The U.S. State Department warns people do not travel here for
risk of kidnapping and terrorism, and advises those who must to leave behind a DNA sample so they can be identified by their family.
[00:50:00]
A lot of the migrants have told us that they are terrified to be here, and you can understand why. I mean, this is a country where there's been an ongoing civil war that's still taking place. But even here in Bangui, there's very high rates of violence, very high rates of criminality, and very high rates of you know these armed groups operating here.
I asked the police about the migrants' claims that they are part of the problem. Some of the migrants here that I've spoken to say that when they go outside, the police stop them and ask them for cash and threaten them.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE (through translator): No, no, those police officers are checking who those people are. I don't believe they're asking them for money.
YEUNG: Very little is known about what terms the CAR government agreed to with the U.S. but what we do know is that on May 18th, just one month before the first deportation flight, State Department diplomats traveled here to meet with the president. For the past decade, the U.S. has given just a few million dollars per year to the U.N. Migration Agency, IOM's operations in CAR, but this year that figure shot up to $85 million.
An additional $50 million was committed to the CAR Humanitarian Fund. We asked the IOM about the money they're receiving. They did not respond to our specific questions, but told us that IOM has a long- standing presence in the CAR and they're receiving funding from a range of donors, but this is at a time when the Trump Administration has been slashing billions of dollars in foreign assistance.
We have been trying to get hold of any U.S. officials to talk about what's been happening and why and how they've sent these migrants here, because they are ultimately the ones responsible. I mean, the State Department have emailed back saying, as a policy, we do not discuss diplomatic negotiations or funding mechanisms, and the U.S. embassy staff have not been replying to us.
But we're going to try a knock on the door just to see if they answer. So, we just have some questions about the migrants that were brought here.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: All questions have to go through them. I'm not authorized to answer any questions.
YEUNG: All right. And is there -- is there anyone else we can contact at the U.S. Embassy?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: The Bangui PD at state.gov the email I gave you. YEUNG: Who are just going to ignore us and pretend that email never
came. All right. Thank you. Eventually, the State Department did get back to us. They did not reply to any of our detailed questions, but said the U.S. will use foreign assistance to support countries that help us achieve our immigration priorities. It's a tacit admission that the money is tied to the deportations.
The U.S. government does have a responsibility towards these migrants. Most of those we spoke to here have protection orders from American judges stopping the government from sending them to their home countries, where they're likely to face persecution, torture, or death.
Fidele Gouandjika is a Special Advisor to the CAR's President. He also hosts a local radio show. As you know, nothing happens in this country without little sweetener. It's a little bit difficult to believe that zero money was given by the U.S. to Central African Republic. I mean, this is one of the most corrupt countries in the world, as you know.
FIDELE GOUANDJIKA, SPECIAL ADVISOR TO CAR'S PRESIDENT (through translator): I do not believe the government could be corrupt I would not want to think for a single second that the Americans or Donald Trump are acting as corruptors.
YEUNG: For Gouandjika, what's happened to these migrants is a sign of American cruelty. When a powerful country like the U.S. comes knocking and asking for this help. Is it difficult for a country like yours to refuse?
GOUANDJIKA (through translator): What saddens me about the United States, a superpower with all the resources could keep these migrants there and they send them here to us instead. The United States is a superpower, but it is a power without a good heart.
YEUNG: Perhaps most concerning of all are those who have nowhere left to run to, like this young pro-democracy activist from Iran, who says she faced possible execution for partaking in protests. Initially, she was overjoyed when she arrived in the U.S.
UNIDENTIFIED FEMALE: I was too much happy that America, one of the best country in the world, and I say it's OK, now I'm safe, and I can make one new life here. I know if I -- I -- I was in Iran, they going to kill me.
YEUNG: Earlier this year, Trump himself encouraged Iranians to keep protesting, promising help is on the way.
UNIDENTIFIED FEMALE: Every time I think they going to protect me, but America not protect me. When they send me here, really, I lose my hope again.
YEUNG: Isobel Yeung, CNN, Central African Republic.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
[00:55:00] ABEL: Nashville's Grand Ole Opry honoring country music icon and humanitarian hero Dolly Parton. The venue says Saturday Night Show was dedicated to recognizing Dolly's life, legacy, and contributions to country music. Several country artists performed, including Carly Pearce, who began her career at Parton's Dollywood theme park, and Parton inducted her into the Grand Ole Opry.
Parton was 13 years old when she made her first appearance at the Grand Ole Opry stage in 1959, introduced by the legendary Jonny Cash. She became an official member by age 23. Following her passing on Tuesday, her name was placed front and center on the venue's outdoor marquee.
Thank you for watching CNN Newsroom. I'm Brian Abel. I'll be right back with more of your top headlines.

ABEL: Rescue teams are racing to find survivors following catastrophic flash floods along Nepal's border with China. Crews from India, China and South Korea are now joining the response, bringing critical expertise and resources to strengthen relief efforts. Nepal's army says it's deployed more than 8,000 service members to help.
About 3,000 people are still missing, but a sweeping rescue effort has saved the lives of nearly 3,000 more. Dramatic new video shows one of those rescues, a young girl trapped under a collapsed building.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
UNIDENTIFIED SPEAKERS: (Foreign Language)
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABEL: Crews took her to a camp that evening. She is now receiving treatment, and authorities say she told rescuers her name is Manisha (ph) and she's just six years old. The death toll continues to climb and authorities are struggling to cope with the mounting number of bodies. Seven hundred and 34 people are now confirmed dead in Nepal and at least 16 others in China. Meanwhile, officials are warning that water levels have surged in a river near the flood zone, raising fears of further flooding. They sent a text to residents asking them to be alert, but did not ask them to evacuate.
Joining me now is CNN's Kristie Lu Stout for us in Hong Kong.
And Kristie, as rescue efforts enter their fifth day, what's the latest on the search for the missing?
KRISTIE LU STOUT, CNN ANCHOR: Yes, Brian the scale of the loss and devastation is coming into harsh focus as search operations resume in the disaster zone and authorities there are struggling. They're also dealing with very difficult conditions like rain and fog and also struggling with the sheer scale of it all. Around 750 people are dead, 3,000 people are missing, and that includes around 900 hydropower workers.
I want to show you this video. It's on your screen right now, provided by the army of Nepal, showing the urgent rescue work underway in regards to these tunnels. What is happening right now is rescuers, they are pumping air into these tunnels in an effort to drill a manhole, to send rescue teams down to be able to get there and to find the missing and to look for any, any survivors. This is an urgent effort that's happening at this very moment.
Late on Saturday night, a statement was issued by authorities. Let's bring it up for you. This is a statement that was brought up from Nepal's Minister of Energy, Water Resources, Irrigation, saying this, quote, "There has been some success in the ongoing rescue efforts at the Trishuli-3, a hydropower project tunnel, which was buried under mud and sludge following the flood," unquote. So encouraging news there is progress, but authorities also say they have yet to establish any communication with any of the trapped workers down there.

But how things will pan out? Who knows? That's it.

STOUT: For survivors like her, they face the daunting task of trying to rebuild their lives. Says it desperately needs that aid to rebuild transport lines like bridges, to rebuild communication lines, as well as technical expertise.
It needs tunnel experts in order to locate the survivors, if there are any there to locate the missing that are under this hydroelectric plant, as well as technical expertise to identify bodies and to conduct DNA tests as well as cold storage for the remains of the victims. The United States has pledged $3.6 million in aid, but that is just a fraction of what Nepal desperately needs right now. Brian.
ABEL: All right, CNN's Kristie Lu Stout for us in Hong Kong. Kristie, thank you.
And Hanako Montgomery made it into a devastated village as the search continues for survivors. As you'll see in her report, Hanako witnessed scenes of heartbreaking destruction.

HANAKO MONTGOMERY, CNN INTERNATIONAL CORRESPONDENT (voice-over): Here in Nepal's Devighat, destruction stretches for miles. Daily life has grounded to a halt here, replaced instead by a steady stream of police and dogs sniffing for any bodies.
MONTGOMERY: And actually, just moments ago, they did find one. We of course can't show it to you, but they told us that because it was so badly decomposed, they couldn't even identify who it was.
MONTGOMERY (voice-over): Downstream, workers are digging a mass grave for the hundreds of bodies that have washed ashore delivered by the truckload. Some are so heavily waterlogged, workers struggle under their weight. And among these bodies is this five-year-old girl. Serina's (ph) family said she was on her way to school when the flood swept her bus away.
We spoke to her father Rajkumar (ph) by phone. He barely survived and is now stranded with help out of reach.
MONTGOMERY: When did you learn, sir, that your daughter was missing?
RAJKUMAR, DAUGHTER DIED IN FLASH FLOOD (voice-over): I was working and I called immediately after I escaped. I was still hoping that my daughter was alive. One of the girls who tried saving her told me she wasn't able to hold her and the flood swept her away.
MONTGOMERY: I can't imagine the pain you must be feeling right now. How are you dealing with this immense tragedy?
RAJKUMAR (voice-over): My dream was always to educate my daughter. So she could be successful in life. That's why I worked so hard. But my dream for her hasn't come true.
MONTGOMERY: But Rajkumar says he can't identify her body with no money or means to confirm that his only child is now gone. Numbers can't describe the human cost of this tragedy. A pain shared by too many here.
Sadip Nepal lost his business in the flood and his home. The one he's lived in for years.
MONTGOMERY: How long do you think it's going to take for you to rebuild all of this?
SADIP NEPAL, SURVIVED FLOOD: I think it is not possible to stay here. I think we have to leave this place.
MONTGOMERY (voice-over): Sadip barely made it out alive when he saw the flood rushing toward his town he said like a tsunami. He ran up a nearby mountain where he filmed these videos. He said all he had was a T-shirt and sandals. But luckily his whole family made it out in time. He shows us inside his home where furniture and a bathroom once stood are now tree branches, ropes and animal carcasses that the river brought in.
Now, more than three days after the flood swept through the valley, the chances of finding trapped survivors alive are getting slimmer. Many of these operations are now becoming search and recovery. A mounting toll of heartbreak for thousands of families.
Hanako Montgomery, CNN Nepal.

And sir, thank you very much for taking your precious time to be with us. It's heartbreaking as this 72 hour window passes for the potential for more survivors, but also heartbreaking for those who have survived with such devastation around them and with the difficult terrain. NARAYAN GYAWALI, NEPAL PROGRAM DIRECTOR, CORUS INTERNATIONAL: Yes, thank you, Brian. As being the humanitarian organization, we immediately reached to the affected program areas. My team, Corus International team, we moved to the Trishuli and to the Bidur municipalities of Nuwakot immediately.

In the six months since the U.S. and Israel launched the war with Iran, the financial and economic impacts of the conflict have reverberated across the world. Since the war began, the U.S. has burned through weapons stockpiles and the price of gas has soared. But none of the original goals laid out by President Donald Trump or Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu have been achieved. CNN Jerusalem Bureau Chief Oren Liebermann has more.
OREN LIEBERMANN, JERUSALEM BUREAU CHIEF: It was six months ago that the U.S. and Israel launched the opening strikes of the war against Iran. And now, as we sit here half a year later, that war very much ongoing and unresolved. President Donald Trump and Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu laid out what seemed to be clear goals for the war, destroy Iran's nuclear program, end its ballistic missile program and end its support for regional proxies.
[01:25:10]
Now, however, with none of that accomplished, the Trump administration has shifted to a different goal and a different strategy. Iran used the war and learned throughout the war that they really didn't need a military able to compete with the U.S. or Israel to hold hostage the Strait of Hormuz and closed down one of the world's most critical waterways, through which 20 percent of the world's oil flows. That became Iran's most powerful weapon. And that is what the U.S. is trying to open right now so that there aren't a global ripple effect of impacts from the closed or limited Strait of Hormuz, and of course, so that there aren't incredibly high gas prices in the U.S. ahead of the midterms. That is the only goal it seems that the U.S. is focusing on right now, trying to make sure the Strait of Hormuz is open and free for traffic.
It is limited here. The Trump administration is trying to boast that it's open for traffic. And yet the traffic through the strait that it's talking about has had to travel through the strait with its transponder off and with some level of U.S. Navy escort or monitoring to make that happen. And that's because even after the U.S. and Israel destroyed much of Iran's navy and air force and military, they're still able to leverage that power on the Strait of Hormuz with drones with short range rockets. And frankly, shipping companies and insurers aren't willing to take that risk.
And so we're in a state now where Iran has shown that it has ability to exert pressure on the Strait of Hormuz without a final resolution here. Meanwhile, the Trump administration has said in recent days that there are no negotiations with Iran, that even as Qatar is trying to negotiate with Iran and Oman to try to find some sort of resolution on the strait itself. And that remains the challenge here, finding a solution that both satisfies the U.S. and Iran. Despite President Donald Trump repeatedly claiming that Iran is begging for a deal, Iran has repeatedly shown that even with the economic pressure they're facing, the inflation and all of the other problems, they're not begging for a deal and they're willing to make very high demands to open up the strait for the world's oil to flow through it.
Oren Liebermann, CNN, in Jerusalem.
