ALLISON CHINCHAR, CNN METEOROLOGIST: The last thing this area needs is the potential for additional flooding. Unfortunately there are rain showers expected in the forecast over the next several days. You can see through Sunday afternoon and Sunday evening we get another round of showers that makes its way through same thing for Monday and even into Tuesday. Both of those days are likely going to have breaks in the morning before we start to see activity pick back up in the afternoon and evening hours which is really kind of the main timeline for this general area because we are right in the middle of monsoon season. August is the second wettest month of the year and for many of these areas, they'll likely pick up 50 to 100 millimeters a day over the next few days.
[01:35:19]
That's roughly two to four inches. So again, you're talking about a lot of rain and a lot of that starts to build back up, bringing more rain to a lot of those river valleys that you've seen in many of the images and videos. The big concern too is also where a lot of the mud flows have occurred. That area, when it hardens, turns to an almost concrete like material. But once you add water back into the mix from rain showers, it liquefies, making it very unstable and very dangerous for a lot of the rescue and the recovery crews to come in.
Not to mention it just continues to cause more messes and potentially increase the likelihood of additional flooding.

We are hearing from the new King of Norway for the first time since the death of his father. King Harald died Friday after more than three decades on the throne. Norwegians have gathered outside of the royal palace where King Harald is lying in state. He died on Friday and at age 89.
The somber outpouring has showed how respected the former king was. He was Europe's oldest monarch who came to the throne after the death of his father who was a well-loved extrovert. Harald became a reformer during his time on the throne. And his death came the day before what would have been his 58th wedding anniversary. He's survived by his wife Sonja.
New King, Haakon VII, spoke about his loss on Saturday. He paid homage to his father who he said was a good dad and became emotional as he congratulated his mother on what should have been a celebration of that anniversary. He also said he looks forward to his new role as king.

May God protect our country.

The family of a deported father and his five-year-old son are speaking out after Mexican authorities said the pair are back in Mexico. U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement detained Victor Martinez Nieto and son Liam in Texas. Video of the detainment was posted to social media. Martinez Nieto's family says he had little choice but to be sent back to Guanajuato with his son. CNN's Valeria Leon reports.
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
VALERIA LEON, CNN CORRESPONDENT (voice-over): Nineteen-year-old Fatima Martinez was working in the fields in this remote town in central Mexico when she learned her older brother Victor Martinez had been detained by ICE in Texas.
FATIMA MARTINEZ, VICTOR'S SISTER (through translator): It makes you sad, you start crying. It's painful to see your brother suffer.
LEON (voice-over): The moment of Victor's arrest was caught on video showing him in chains with his five-year-old Liam crying by his side. Facing the possibility of being separated from Liam, Victor agreed to be deported to Mexico.
LEON: Victor Martinez and his son Liam were sent back to his parents home here in this small town in the Mexican state of Guanajuato, surrounded by mountains. Most of the people here harvest corn and this is the exact place where Victor decided to leave to the U.S. Now his family welcomes him back.
[01:50:01]
MARTINEZ (through translator): It's good to see him again, but at the same time it's sad because they only go their to pursue their dreams, but they don't let them.
LEON (voice-over): Fatima is the eldest of 12 siblings. Four of them are still in the U.S. Victor had crossed the border earlier this year, a journey his family says came at a very high price. Fatima told CNN he still owes $15,000 to the smuggler who helped him cross into the U.S.
MARTINEZ (through translator): Here, we basically survive by working in the fields. We're a little forgotten.
LEON (voice-over): Getting to Puerto de Tablas, where the family lives, is not easy. After driving through curves, rocks and dirt, we arrived at the community. There we met Victor's aunt, Guadalupe. She knows what it means to have a family leave. Two decades ago, her eldest son left for the U.S. when he was just 15.
She says he never came back. For Victor, she hopes things will be different.
GUADALUPE MARTINEZ, VICTOR'S AUNT: Poort thing, I hope he finds a job here, something that helps him move forward. Because there's nowhere to work here.
LEON (voice-over): Now Victor is back in the place he once left, searching for opportunities elsewhere.
Valeria Leon, CNN, Guanajuato, Mexico.
(END VIDEO CLIP)

MIKE BALL, CHIEF OPERATING OFFICER, ALL HANDS & HEARTS: Yeah. Thank you for having me. It's a really, really challenging -- excuse me, challenging situation here. Essentially, the worst affected communities are being reached by the Army in search and rescue. I think this is the biggest challenge of Nepal and the terrain. As you've seen from the event and the footage, roads are destroyed. Bridges are out. So, it's incredibly difficult for anybody to get to the truly affected areas where search and rescue is happening.
Where we are at the moment is by the Army barracks in Bara, and in Nuwakot, and the devastation we've seen here is also unbelievable. So, the area that we've been to Devighat, which is near here, is completely decimated. So, this is 50 kilometers downstream from where the flooding started, and it's like a tsunami has been through. In all honesty, it must be 20 to 30 feet of water coming through, 200, 300 meters wide. So, we've spoke to people here about the devastation, and -- yeah. So, this is just a snapshot that we're seeing to what'll be 100 times over, as you keep going up the river.
HUNTE: Your team is supporting the Army's search and rescue operation. What are the rescuers experiencing, as they search for people that are still missing?
BALL: It's just an incredibly difficult situation, I think, for everybody, again, if you imagine what's being left here with the size of the water and the mud and the debris. It's monsoon season. So, you still have the rain. You have the threat of potentially more flooding coming later. So, it's incredibly challenging conditions to try and find people there and people trapped in the inside buildings full of merge and rocks. We heard about the hydro plant and people trapped in the tunnel. So, it's a real mix of, I think, of conditions.
[02:10:00]
But the level of mud and debris, and trying to rescue people from inside buildings in these conditions, is incredibly, incredibly challenging.

They're actually moving people to Bara and then they're putting them into holding centers or displacement centers at the moment. So, one of our key focuses is making sure that we can get sanitary items, hygiene products, clothing, food, water, anything that people need when they've lost everything. They've been relocated. They've lost their homes. We've heard stories of people about to walk five or six hours out of the affected area to reach a center to get to the Army to then be relocated here.
So, this area is growing in the number of centers and the number of people who are being placed here. So, there is a humanitarian crisis, I guess, around here in terms of making sure that there is enough supplies for everybody here, which is separate to the search and rescue, and everything happening further north as well. So, this is on a massive scale in many different contexts, areas going on.

It is years and years and years of recovery. So, I think the -- I know governments are stepping up now with a level of funding. This will need years and years and years of support by the Nepali government and by international community to help. So, it's coming in.
But again, I think we're only seeing a snapshot of what's happening, and I think until we can really reach the affected areas, until everybody can get in, it's hard to see exactly what's needed at the moment.

We want to seek help from public, government, military, I don't know who is going to help me, but if you guys can start looking for them, because there are some of them who needs their medical attention on a day-to-day basis. I also have one of my elderly brother who had joined there, and he is 70 plus, super fit. Many of them run their marathons. But of course, see, they are not ready for something like this, and as I said, yes, they need their medications on a timely basis. They, of course, need to take their food. I don't know what not, but 80 hours is way too long for them to be somewhere where they are not taken care of.
(END VIDEOTAPE)
HUNTE: There is much more to come here on CNN Newsroom.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
HUNTE: Welcome back. The flood disaster along the Nepal-China border was not caused by infrastructure, but it is highlighting a stark reality in the Himalayas. The risks of natural disasters in the region are growing due to climate change. At the same time, the countries have been ramping up infrastructure projects. They have been rapidly expanding dams, tunnels, highways, railways, and bridges too, as they aim to harness the region's vast hydropower potential and resources.
[02:35:00]
Joining me now is CNN's Senior China Reporter Simone McCarthy. Simone, there has been a lot of misinformation claiming that infrastructure caused this disaster. But what do we actually know about what happened, and where did those claims come from?
SIMONE MCCARTHY, CNN SENIOR CHINA REPORTER: Well, exactly. Certainly, the disaster that we're dealing with on the China-Nepal border was not caused by infrastructure. There were certainly rumors that were circulating at that time. The Chinese embassy in Nepal has come out and said that's fake news, and the scientific community has said that this disaster was caused by a glacier which broke off and then triggered a massive landslide as well as flooding. So, that is what we know about how this incident happened, this absolute catastrophe at the border.
But what this is shining a light on at the same time is that there has been this real buildout of infrastructure across the Himalayas, and that's led by China, in many respects, in terms of the most ambitious and kind of engineering -- complex from an engineering perspective projects, but also India and as well as Nepal. A lot of this is about hydropower. Countries want to wean off of coal. They have increasing demand for electricity. And so, they're looking to those high-altitude rivers in the Himalayas in order to be able to harness electricity power there to really electrify their countries, but at the same time, having more projects in the region, especially at a time when there is so much volatility there.
This is really one of the most seismically active parts of the world. It's a place that's prone to natural disaster, as we've just catastrophically seen in recent days. And climate change is really raising those risks, and it's also making them more unpredictable. And so, that's raising a lot of questions about, OK, well, if we have more infrastructure projects in the region, what can we do to mitigate those risks? Certainly, today we've seen a lot of rescue efforts in Nepal are focused around workers who are working in hydropower projects that are now missing, and that really highlights just what we're talking about here, Ben.
Now, the sense is it's hydropower. There is also economic development that's driving some of this infrastructure boom, and certainly frictions along the China-India border, where those two countries have a long simmering border -- excuse me, long simmering border frictions, and that's also encouraging both countries to ensure that they're able to mobilize there. So, that's roads. We've seen new tunnels being built out on the Indian side.
And so, while, of course, we know that this disaster was not caused by infrastructure, having more projects in the region raises questions about, OK, well, when there are these kinds of natural disasters which are becoming more unpredictable, are these projects really equipped to be able to handle that?
Now, Chinese engineers are seen to be among the best in the world, and they really focus on mitigation and surveillance. But a number of the experts that I've been talking to in recent days, Ben, what they're saying is so much is changing so quickly in the region as a result of climate change that, yes, we can plan for the reality that we know now, but are we going to be able to plan for the risks in the future, and what happens when these projects perhaps aren't able to hold up to that risk, and then put people and the projects themselves in harm's way.
HUNTE: OK. CNN's Simone McCarthy in Beijing, thank you very much.
There is much more to come here on CNN Newsroom. Country music's biggest stage is paying tribute to Dolly Parton. We'll have more on the Grand Ole Opry's dedication just ahead.

HUNTE: Nashville's Grand Ole Opry is honoring country music icon and humanitarian hero Dolly Parton. The venue says Saturday night's show was dedicated to Dolly's life, legacy, and contributions to country music. Several country artists performed, including Carly Pearce, who began her career at Dollywood Theme Park. Parton was 13-years-old when she made her first appearance on the Grand Ole Opry stage in 1959, introduced by the legendary Johnny Cash. She became an official member by age 23. Following her passing on Tuesday, her name was placed front and center on the venue's outdoor marquee.
Joining us now is Lydia Hamessley. She is the author of "Unlikely Angel: The Songs of Dolly Parton", and is professor of musk at Hamilton College. Thank you so much for being with me. Tributes to Dolly are happening across the country and all around the world too. But what is it about her music that makes it resonate with so many people?
LYDIA HAMESSLEY, PROFESSOR OF MUSIC, HAMILTON COLLEGE: I think it's probably that she is such a great storyteller. She pulls you in right from the very beginning of a song, and she also has the gift of melody and very catchy tunes. And so, when you combine the great songs -- excuse me, the great stories with the melodies that people can sing along with so easily, like Jolene, you can't help but connect with people. I think it's her storytelling, though, most of all.
HUNTE: The Grand Ole Opry dedicated an entire show to Dolly. What does the Opry mean to her legacy, and what does it mean to see the country music community come together like this?
HAMESSLEY: Well, it's really terrific, and of course, it means so much to her legacy because it meant so much to her as a kid dreaming of being on the Opry, and the way that she kind of managed to even talk her way onto it when she was technically even too young to appear. It sort of puts her in perspective of that. That's the kind of ambition that she had, that she was just going to get out there on that stage no matter what.
[02:50:00]
And of course, they just did the 50th anniversary of her having joined the Opry a few years ago, and did a big show for that. And this evening, at the end of the current dedication, they played the ending of that 50th anniversary of hers, in which she sang "I Will Always Love You. So, that closed out her own celebration for 50 years, but it closed out this dedication, which was really poignant to see her singing that about, please don't cry and I have to go. I mean, it really resonated so differently now in light of her passing.
HUNTE: You have written an entire book about Jolene. Why has that particular song become such a massive piece of American music?
HAMESSLEY: Well, partly what I said about the story and the catchiness, but I think also because it's kind of different. It's in a minor key. And so, it's got a little bit of a haunting quality to it. Dolly was asked the question herself. What's up with Jolene? Why is that such a great song? And she said, well, because it's so simple that anybody could sing along with it. She sang the first Jolene, Jolene, Jolene, Jolene. She said even a baby can sing that. I'm not a genius.
But the other thing that she said about that song was that it was about feeling inferior, and that everybody could relate to that, that everybody has the sense that somebody else has something on them, or they could take away their partner, or they're better than them somehow. And so, whether it's the situation that's specific to Jolene, or it's just the sense of feeling insecure, there is a way that that song captures that, and yet I think in that chorus with the name going over and over, it also feels a little bit confident. Like, yeah, I'm worried about this, but I also am coming to you face-to-face and singing your name and challenging you a little bit.
HUNTE: Dolly built an incredible career in an industry dominated by men. How much did she change the possibilities for women in country music?
HAMESSLEY: Well, I think quite a bit. She always said that -- growing up in a family, she had three or four older brothers, three older brothers, and a bunch of younger brothers, and lots of uncles, and she said, I grew up around men. I understood men. I knew how to handle them. So, when she got the inevitable misogynistic sexist stuff that she would have gotten back in the 60s and 70s, she knew how to disarm it without being -- making people feel bad about what they had been doing. So, there is that part of it.
But I think also she has often reached a hand out to other women and helped them along the way. And of course, tonight's celebration was initially the show to celebrate Carly Pearce's fifth anniversary on the Grand Ole Opry, and they showed the clip with Dolly entering, much to Carly's surprise, to be a part of that invitation for her to join. And Dolly has helped women many times along the way; reaching out; singing with them, doing duets with them. There was a recent one, I'm sorry, I can't remember the artist's name, but she wrote a song that was kind of riffing on the idea of Jolene, Jolene's son, and Dolly heard that and said, wow, that's a pretty cool idea. I'll sing that with you. I mean, that certainly didn't help someone's career.
HUNTE: Absolutely incredible stories. Lydia Hamessley, thank you so much. We appreciate your time. Thank you for being with us.
HAMESSLEY: Thank you.
HUNTE: People who worked with Dolly in her movies are also talking about the impact that she had on their lives. Actress, producer, and New York Times best-selling author Danica McKellar is looking back at her time on set with Dolly in the 2019 movie "Christmas at Dollywood."
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
DANICA MCKELLAR, ACTRESS, PRODUCER & NYT BEST-SELLING AUTHOR (on camera): There is a documentary made about the Imagination Library, and I was asked to narrate that earlier in the year. And at the recording session, who stops in to surprise me? But Dolly Parton herself. I was floored. I was just -- my -- I was gobsmacked. My mouth dropped open. There she was, head to toe, black leather with grommet holes and like a big chain cross, and just her hair, and all her glory. It was an amazing moment that I'll never forget.
And then later that year, we did this movie together.

Working with her was incredible. She has a really strong work ethic. She wanted to rehearse, and I would love to rehearse. Let's rehearse all day. She was just a sparkling ball of light. We were all -- I was nervous about doing this big scene with her. I hadn't been nervous about doing an acting scene. I've been acting for like 40 years, but I had all these butterflies, and she walked on the set. We were all just so nervous. She walked on the set, and she goes, Merry Christmas, everybody. And we all just -- it just diffused all of the tension and the nervousness we all had. She put us all at ease immediately. She is wearing head to toe silver sparkles with these six-inch heels platform, just incredible, and she was amazing.
And no matter what she said, it sounded good. I still remember that the director of photography said, Ms. Parton, you're going to be stepping over some cords here, and she is wearing these tall heels, right? I don't want you to trip over them, and she goes, no, you don't, honey. It was just so charming. And so, she knew how to deliver any kind of message with charm. She makes everyone feel good about themselves. She knows exactly what to say.
I think it was the wardrobe woman who made a self-deprecating remark about how she can ever be as beautiful as Dolly, and it's kind of an awkward position to be in, right? BEN HUNTE, CNN ANCHOR: Hello and welcome. I'm Ben Hunte in Atlanta.
Ahead here on CNN Newsroom, hopes of finding more survivors in Nepal are growing more desperate as search and rescue operations enter their fourth day.
Ukraine launches a criminal investigation after Russia unleashes one of its deadliest attacks since the war began.
And it's been six months since the U.S. and Israel went to war against Iran, with no resolution in sight.
Welcome. We begin with fears of further flooding along the Nepal-China border. Officials are warning that water levels have surged in a river near the area where flooding hit early this week. They sent a text to residents asking them to be alert, but did not ask them to evacuate.
Right now, an urgent search is underway for workers who may be trapped in hydropower tunnels. Rescuers are pumping air into one of the tunnels and plan to drill a manhole, hoping to send teams inside to find any survivors. Officials say they are already sending in oxygen and food. Specialized teams from India and China have now arrived in Nepal to help.
The U.S. is significantly expanding its support, pledging $3.6 million in humanitarian aid as communities grapple with the devastation left by the floods. That is a sharp increase from the $500,000 the U.S. first announced days ago.
About three thousand people are still missing, but a sweeping rescue effort has saved the lives of nearly three thousand more. Dramatic new video shows just one of those rescues, a young girl trapped under a collapsed building.
Joining me now is CNN's Kristie Lu Stout in Hong Kong. Kristie, what is the latest you're hearing?
KRISTIE LU STOUT, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Ben, there is immense devastation and loss. Rescue efforts and search efforts continue for yet another day in the disaster zone. They're also struggling with very challenging weather conditions in their rescue and search operations, including fog, rain, rising levels of water. And, of course, you have the sheer scale of it all. Around 750 people are dead. 3,000 people are missing, among them, at least 900 people work in these hydropower plants.
This video that you're seeing on your screen right now was given to us by the army in Nepal. And in it, we see a rescue effort underway in one of those tunnels that they were able to access as they tried to search and find for any survivors.
But in a separate tunnel, rescuers are just trying to access that tunnel at another hydropower plant, where they are trying to pump air down, and they're sending food down as well. The plan is to drill some sort of a manhole so they can send more rescue teams down there.
We do have an update on that rescue effort. This was provided by a statement that was released late last night. Let's bring it up for you. This according to Nepal's Minister of Energy, Water Resources, and Irrigation, who shared this, quote, that there has been some success in the ongoing rescue efforts in the Trishuli-3 A hydropower project tunnel, which was buried under mud and sludge following the flood, unquote.
[03:05:03]
So, progress on that front, but they have yet to establish any sort of communication with any workers who may still be alive and trapped there.
Now, meanwhile, the families of the missing, some of them still holding out hope, the agonizing wait for them continues. Many of them are even going to the morgue to see if they can get any sort of closure, any sort of answers about what happened to their loved ones.
Survivors, meanwhile, are waiting for desperately needed aid to arrive, aid like food, like clean water, hygiene supplies. Many of the roads in the area are impassable since Wednesday's disaster. And some survivors have also managed to return to try to collect their belongings at what was their home, only to see that nothing is there.

STOUT: For both the entire nation of Nepal and its people, they face the daunting task of rebuilding their lives, and the finance minister of Nepal told Reuters in an interview that the country needs $4 to $5 billion in aid that is needed to rebuild roads, transport links, bridges, as well as communication lines, aid that is need for tunnel experts to locate the missing, aid that is also needed for technical experts to help identify, using DNA analysis, the remains of the victims, as well as to store the recovered bodies.
And we know that the United States has pledged $3.6 million worth of aid to give to Nepal, but Nepal, even before this disaster, was already an impoverished and very poor nation. That amount of aid pledged by the U.S. is just a fraction of what it truly needs right now. Ben?
HUNTE: Okay. I have CNN's Kristie Lu Stout in Hong Kong, thank you for joining us.

MIKE BALL, COO, ALL HANDS AND HEARTS: It's a really, really challenging -- excuse me -- challenging situation here. Essentially, the worst affected communities are being reached by the army in search and rescue. I think this is the biggest challenge of Nepal and the terrain. As you've seen from the event and the footage, roads are destroyed, bridges are out, so it's incredibly difficult for anybody to get to the truly affected areas where search and rescue is happening.
Where we are at the moment is by the army barracks in Bharat in Nuwakot. And with the devastation we've seen here is also unbelievable. So, the area that we've been to in Debighat, which is near here, is completely decimated. So, this is 50 kilometers downstream from where the flooding started and it's like a tsunami has been through, in all honesty. Essentially, it's paralyzed and there are no new protocols to address this new challenge. Russia knows it and Vladimir Putin once again remains fixated on bringing Ukrainians to their knees, whether through a military victory, but it's not in the cards of course. But this way, you know, what we're seeing is he's trying to make life in Ukrainian cities and towns impossible.
HUNTE: President Zelenskyy says those responsible must be held accountable. What do you know so far about the investigation and who could possibly face consequences?
ZALMAYEV: Well, I think the buck stops with the president. Once again, it's not the first time it happened. You know, he has let his former defense minister go, and now he's got a new set of people in place. It would be unfeasible, of course, to expect Zelenskyy to let them go this early into their tenure.
And it is indeed unclear when it comes to the investigation, whether it was a hit from the air or if it was a sabotage. That means that there are moles, you know, informers from within the Ukrainian military that, in fact, share this information with the enemy. And if that is the case, then the rot has spread significantly.
And, once again, we're hoping that the investigation is transparent, but this cannot be business as usual, you know, because that leaves Ukrainians extremely vulnerable, of course. And in the absence of sufficient air defenses, Ukraine, of course, cannot afford to lose such significant caches of weaponry, not to speak of the significant cost in civilian lives that such, you know, sabotage, if that's what it is, leads to.
HUNTE: With hundreds of people being evacuated, including from nearby homes and a care facility, what are people who stay in the area facing right now?
ZALMAYEV: Well, people are facing a very stark choice, and it's not just in this area, but throughout Ukraine. Not just small towns, but major towns. They're facing a choice as they're, you know, near approaching the fifth winter of war, which is promising to be the coldest winter on record. They're facing a choice whether they stay, hunker down, resist, you know, or they move out, you know?
And that's once again another of Vladimir Putin's calculations. You know, imagine what it would entail an evacuation of a city like Kyiv of 3 to 4 million people. That is a tremendous humanitarian catastrophe. And it is easy to assume that a lot of those people will be forced to leave Ukraine and pour across the borders of Ukraine, thereby exacerbating further the situation in some of these countries, especially Poland, where, you know, the relationship -- the relations between the Poles and the Ukrainians, the refugee, Ukrainian refugees has already been strained.
Vladimir Putin is very much aware of it, so he's trying his hardest to, once again, make life of Ukrainians impossible, hard to exacerbate the situations in some of these countries that have so, you know, graciously hosted hundreds of thousands and even millions of Ukrainian refugees in the last five, almost five years.
HUNTE: Okay. Peter Zalmayev, thank you very much for speaking to us, and speak to you again soon.
ZALMAYEV: You're welcome.
HUNTE: In the six months since the U.S. and Israel launched the war with Iran, the financial and economic impact of the conflict has been felt all across the world. Since the war began, the U.S. has burned through weapons stockpiles, and the price of gas has soared. But none of the original goals laid out by President Donald Trump or Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu have been achieved.
CNN's Oren Liebermann has more for us.
OREN LIEBERMANN, CNN JERUSALEM BUREAU CHIEF: It was six months ago that the U.S. and Israel launched the opening strikes of the war against Iran. And now, as we sit here half a year later, that war very much ongoing and unresolved.
President Donald Trump and Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu laid out what seemed to be clear goals for the war: destroy Iran's nuclear pl- program, end its ballistic missile program and end its support for regional proxies. Now, however, with none of that accomplished, the Trump administration has shifted to a different goal and a different strategy.
Iran used the war and learned throughout the war that they really didn't need a military able to compete with the U.S. or Israel to hold hostage the Strait of Hormuz and close down one of the world's most critical waterways through which 20 percent of the world's oil flows.
[03:25:11]
That became Iran's most powerful weapon, and that is what the U.S. is trying to open right now so that there aren't a global ripple effect of impacts from the closed or limited Strait of Hormuz, and, of course, so that there aren't incredibly high gas prices in the U.S. ahead of the midterms.
That is the only goal, it seems, that the U.S. is focusing on right now, trying to make sure the Strait of Hormuz is open and free for traffic. It is limited here. The Trump administration is trying to boast that it's open for traffic, and yet the traffic through the strait that it's talking about has had to travel through the strait with its transponder off and with some level of U.S. Navy escort or monitoring to make that happen.
And that's because even after the U.S. and Israel destroyed much of Iran's navy and air force and military, they're still able to leverage that power on the Strait of Hormuz with drones, with short-range rockets, and, frankly, shipping companies and insurers aren't willing to take that risk. And so we're in a state now where Iran has shown that it has ability to exert pressure on the Strait of Hormuz without a final resolution here.
Meanwhile, the Trump administration has said in recent days that there are no negotiations with Iran, that even as Qatar is trying to negotiate with Iran and Oman to try to find some sort of resolution on the strait itself, and that remains the challenge here, finding a solution that both satisfies the U.S. and Iran.
Despite President Donald Trump repeatedly claiming that Iran is begging for a deal, Iran has repeatedly shown that even with the economic pressure they're facing, the inflation, and all of the other problems, they're not begging for a deal, and they're willing to make very high demands to open up the strait for the world's oil to flow through it.
Oren Liebermann, CNN in Jerusalem.
HUNTE: An Israeli airstrike reportedly has killed three people in the occupied West Bank. There is much more to come here on CNN Newsroom, including the latest on the recovery efforts in Nepal and China. Families around the world are hoping for any news of their missing loved ones.

HUNTE: Welcome back. I am Ben Hunte in Atlanta.
Let's take a look at today's top stories. Ukraine is conducting a criminal investigation after a Russian strike that left at least 37 people dead. President Volodymyr Zelenskyy says he wants the people who stored the ammunition to be held accountable.
Dozens of people in Northern Italy were hurt after a tornado and hail hit the town of Cremona. Rescue teams responded to more than 500 emergency calls. 10 historic churches in the city were damaged, including the main cathedral, where a spire fell. A dozen families had to be evacuated after their homes were damaged. The city is famous for making instruments and was holding a festival when the storm hit.
750 people have now been confirmed killed by the terrible flooding along the China-Nepal border. Rescue workers are searching for any survivors. More than 3,000 people are still missing, the vast majority of them in Nepal. Hundreds of those unaccounted for are foreigners.
The families of U.S. citizens missing in Nepal and China are waiting desperately for news of their loved ones. Rafael Romo gives us an update.
RAFAEL ROMO, CNN CORRESPONDENT: According to the U.S. State Department, there are about 90 Americans missing after the devastating flood that hit Nepal's border with China on Wednesday. One of those missing Americans is Anil Goel from Virginia. According to his family, Goel, an avid hiker, had planned a multi-day trip to hike Mount Kailash near Nepal's border with China as part of a tour group.
But his son, Anurag Goel, told CNN this morning that the family hasn't heard from his father or the group since the day before the disaster. Anurag Goel says the last communication from the group happened less than an hour before the flooding happened in that area around the border.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
ANURAG GOEL, FATHER ANIL DURAG MISSING IN NEPAL WITH TOUR GROUP: They were known to last be at the Nepal side of the Nepal and Tibet border that's called Gyirong Crossing, and that was -- They were last known to have a, a received their departure stamp on their passports around 8:35 A.M. to depart Nepal, enter Tibet.
And there were also some messages through WhatsApp exchanged around that time. So, that was roughly 45 minutes before the flooding took place in that area.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: Goel also told CNN that, quote, there's been a large lag in communication from the affected region that has made it even more difficult for families, like his, who are desperate for any news about their loved ones.

Leaders from the Long Island Nepalese Society organized the vigil to gather in solidarity, prayer and hope as CNN affiliate News 12 Long Island reported. MYAM JIRI, LONG ISLAND NEPALESE SOCIETY: It's very heartbreaking to all of us to see our fellow country people losing their lives and houses in a matter of a few minutes.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: A State Department spokesperson told CNN the United States government is sending aid to Nepal, including food, and that the first trucks carrying assistance are on the way. The United Kingdom has pledged nearly $6.8 million in assistance, and the European Union said it would provide $2.3 million in aid.
[03:35:08]
Rafael Romo, CNN, Atlanta.
HUNTE: UNICEF says more than 17,000 children are in need of humanitarian assistance after the Nepal China flooding. It is appealing for more than $17 million to help deliver its emergency response. JOE ENGLISH, EMERGENCY COMMUNICATIONS MANAGER, UNICEF: As the situation becomes clearer and the impact becomes clearer, what is becoming clear is this is an incredibly grave situation. And we know that in the weeks and months ahead, the children are going to need support in terms of, you know, protecting them from waterborne diseases and nutrition support and psychosocial support.
But the very real risk at this moment is the continued rain and the development of this second lake, which is being monitored. But that not only poses a risk to the children and the families and the communities who've already been impacted by this, but also the search and rescue teams and the humanitarian teams who are on the ground trying to reach people with these basics of life.
And so it's an incredibly concerning situation. We are buoyed along by the hope of these rescues that we see in your report earlier, and the fact that we have the knowledge of how to provide help to people in these incredibly concerning situations. But it is a huge challenge.
One really critical issue now in terms of child protection is identifying children who may be unaccompanied or separated from their families.
Now, this is incredibly likely. We already know. We've got reports of this, but, you know, this crisis struck, the flood struck at nine o'clock on a school day, so many children were in school. They were not at home. They were not close to their parents. And so we've met with children who were separated and now thankfully have been reunited.
And, you know, as a humanitarian worker, there is no better feeling than when you see a child reunited with their parents. But the flipside of that is that there will be many, many children who have lost parents, and there will be many, many parents who have lost their children.

Yiannopoulos rose to fame as an editor for far-right news organization, Breitbart. He was an outspoken supporter of Donald Trump during his 2016 presidential campaign.
The Trump administration's immigration policy involves so-called third country deportations. That's when migrants are sent from the United States to countries other than their own. Just months ago, ICE sent their first deportation flight to the Central African Republic. That's one of the most dangerous countries on Earth.

At 8:58 P.M. on June 11th, 18 migrants from all over the world were brought to Alexandria Airport, Louisiana. They say they were handcuffed and shackled. Several claim they were severely beaten. A U.S. official called the allegation a flat out lie and denied anyone was beaten. The maker of one such restraint said it's a last resort tool intended to promote safety and prevent escalation.

Maybe I can just start with, where are you from originally?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: I'm from Georgia.

UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Yes.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Egypt.
YEUNG: Egypt.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Afghanistan.
YEUNG: Afghanistan.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Iraq.
YEUNG: Iraq.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Jordan.
YEUNG: Jordan. So, all different countries. How are you all doing here?
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Doing here? Like a hell.

UNIDENTIFIED MALE: Yes. No internet, no electric, too much of mosquito. Everybody have malaria.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: (SPEAKING FOREIGN LANGUAGE).
YEUNG: The U.S. State Department warns people do not travel here for risk of kidnapping and terrorism, and advises those who must to leave behind a DNA sample so they can be identified by their family.
A lot of the migrants have told us that they are terrified to be here, and you can understand why. I mean, this is a country where there's been an ongoing civil war that's still taking place. But even here in Bangui, there's very high rates of violence, very high rates of criminality, and very high rates of, you know, these armed groups operating here.
I asked the police about the migrants' claims that they are part of the problem.
Some of the migrants here that I've spoken to say that when they go outside, the police stop them and ask them for cash and threaten them.
UNIDENTIFIED MALE: No, no. (SPEAKING FOREIGN LANGUAGE).
[03:45:02]
YEUNG: Very little is known about what terms the CAR government agreed to with the U.S. But what we do know is that on May 18th, just one month before the first deportation flight, State Department diplomats traveled here to meet with the president.

We asked the IOM about the money they're receiving. They did not respond to our specific questions but told us that IOM has a longstanding presence in the CAR and they're receiving funding from a range of donors.
But this is at a time when the Trump administration has been slashing billions of dollars in foreign assistance.
We have been trying to get a hold of any U.S. officials to talk about what's been happening and why and how they've sent these migrants here because they are ultimately the ones responsible. UNIDENTIFIED MALE: The Bangui P.D. at state.gov, the email I gave you, that's who you can contact.
YEUNG: Who are just going to ignore us and pretend that email never came. All right, thank you.
Eventually, the State Department did get back to us. They did not reply to any of our detailed questions, but said the U.S. will use foreign assistance to support countries that help us achieve our immigration priorities. Fidele Gouandjika is a special adviser to the CAR's president. He also hosts a local radio show.

FIDELE GOUANDJIKA, SPECIAL ADVISER TO PRESIDENT OF CENTRAL AFRICAN REPUBLIC: (SPEAKING FOREIGN LANGUAGE).

GOUANDJIKA: (SPEAKING FOREIGN LANGUAGE).

YEUNG: Earlier this year, Trump himself encouraged Iranians to keep protesting, promising help is on the way.
UNIDENTIFIED FEMALE: Every time I think they going to protect me, but America not protect me. When they send me here, really, I lose my hope again.

Well, people who worked with Dolly in her movies are also talking about the impact that she had on their lives. Actress, producer, and New York Times bestselling author, Danica McKellar, is looking back at her time on set with Dolly in the 2019 movie, Christmas at Dollywood.

DANICA MCKELLAR, ACTRESS , PRODUCER AND NEW YORK TIMES BEST-SELLING AUTHOR: There's a documentary made about the Imagination Library, and I was asked to narrate that earlier in the year. And at the recording session, who stops in to surprise me, but Dolly Parton herself.

She walked onto set and she goes, Merry Christmas, everybody, and we all just -- it just diffused all of the tension and the nervousness we all had. We were -- she put us all at ease immediately. She's wearing head-to-toe silver sparkles with these, you know, six-inch heels, a platform, just incredible and she was amazing.
And no matter what she said, sounded good. I still remember the director of photography said, Ms. Parton, you're going to be stepping over some cords here. And she's wearing these tall heels, right? [04:00:00]
SALMA ABDELAZIZ, CNN ANCHOR: Hello and a very warm welcome. I'm Salma Abdelaziz in London. Let's go straight to those headlines.
Rescue efforts grow more frantic as the death toll rises from the catastrophic Nepal-China flooding. We're on the ground in Nepal with the latest. And the need for humanitarian assistance is dire. I'll speak with an aid worker on the current conditions and what people need most right now. Plus, Ukraine is reeling from one of the deadliest incidents since Russia launched its war. We're going to explain why President Zelenskyy has started a criminal investigation.
ANNOUNCER: Live from London, this is CNN Newsroom with Salma Abdelaziz.
ABDELAZIZ: Rescue teams are right now racing to find survivors following that devastating flash flooding along Nepal's border with China. Right now, an urgent search is underway for workers who might be trapped in hydropower tunnels.
The death toll does continue to climb and authorities are struggling to cope with the mounting number of bodies. Workers are now preparing a mass grave. This is to bury hundreds of unidentified victims. 734 people are confirmed dead in Nepal and at least 16 others in China.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
SHISIR KHANAL, NEPALI FOREIGN MINISTER: Our immediate priority remains clear to save lives, locate missing persons, rescue them, provide urgent relief to survivors, and ensure that affected Nepali as well as foreign nationals receive appropriate assistance and support.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABDELAZIZ: Teams from India, China, and South Korea are now joining the response, bringing critical expertise and resources. About 3,000 people are still missing, but a sweeping rescue effort has saved the lives of nearly 3,000 more. There is dramatic new video now that shows one of those rescues. It's a young girl trapped under a collapsed building. Take a look. In the meantime, Chinese officials say that a barrier lake has formed by a landslide near the flood zone and has now overflowed, but they say the situation is stable, adding that the possibility of a complete breach is for now relatively low. CNN's Hanako Montgomery made it into a devastated village as the search for those survivors continue. And as you're going to see in her report, Hanako witnessed just scenes of heartbreaking destruction.
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
HANAKO MONTGOMERY, CNN CORRESPONDENT (voice-over): Here in Nepal's Devigat, destruction stretches for miles. Daily life has grounded to a halt here, replaced instead by a steady stream of police and dogs sniffing for any bodies.
MONTGOMERY: And actually, just moments ago, they did find one. We, of course, can't show it to you, but they told us that because it was so badly decomposed, they couldn't even identify who it was.
MONTGOMERY (voice-over): Downstream, workers are digging a mass grave for the hundreds of bodies that have washed ashore, delivered by the truckload. Some are so heavily waterlogged. Workers struggle under their weight.
And among these bodies is this five-year-old girl. Serina's family said she was on her way to school when the flood swept her bus away. We spoke to her father, Rajkumar (ph), by phone. He barely survived and is now stranded with help out of reach.
MONTGOMERY: When did you learn, sir, that your daughter was missing?
RAJKUMAR (PH) (through translator): I was working, and I called immediately after I escaped. I was still hoping that my daughter was alive. One of the girls who tried saving her told me she wasn't able to hold her, and the flood swept her away.
MONTGOMERY: I can't imagine the pain you must be feeling right now. How are you dealing with this immense tragedy?
RAJKUMAR (PH) (through translator): My dream was always to educate my daughter so she could be successful in life. That's why I worked so hard. But my dream for her hasn't come true.
MONTGOMERY (voice-over): But Rajkumar (ph) says he can't identify her body, with no money or means to confirm that his only child is now gone.
Numbers can't describe the human cost of this tragedy, a pain shared by too many here. Sadiq Nepal lost his business in the flood and his home, the one he's lived in for years.
[04:05:00]
MONTGOMERY: How long do you think it's going to take for you to rebuild all of this?
SADIQ NEPAL, SURVIVED FLOOD: I think it is not possible to stay here. I think we are to leave this place.
MONTGOMERY (voice-over): Sadiq barely made it out alive when he saw the flood rushing toward his town, he said, like a tsunami. He ran up a nearby mountain where he filmed these videos. He said all he had was a t-shirt and sandals. But luckily his whole family made it out in time. He shows us inside his home, where furniture and a bathroom once stood are now tree branches, ropes and animal carcasses that the river brought in.
Now, more than three days after the flood swept through the valley, the chances of finding trapped survivors alive are getting slimmer. Many of these operations are now becoming search and recovery, amounting to a toll of heartbreak for thousands of families.
Hanako Montgomery, CNN, Nepal.
(END VIDEOTAPE)
ABDELAZIZ: For more on those search and rescue operations, we're joined now live by Kristie Lu Stout in Hong Kong. As we heard there from Hanako Montgomery, the chances of finding someone alive are becoming slimmer and slimmer, and the challenges for rescue workers keep on mounting. What are you learning right now? About 3,000 people, I believe, that are still missing, still unaccounted for?
KRISTIE LU STOUT, CNN CORRESPONDENT: That's right. Yes, Salma. I mean, we are days after this disaster took place on Wednesday, and yet the death toll is rising. Thousands of people remain missing. The search operation continues for yet another day in the disaster zone, and authorities are struggling. They're struggling to reach remote areas, including those tunnels under those hydroelectric plants, where scores of workers are believed to be missing. They are struggling with very adverse weather conditions, like rain, like fog, like rising water levels.
And, of course, you have the sheer scale of it all. 750 people at least are dead. Some 3,000 people are missing. Among those, around 900 are those missing hydroelectric workers. And we have this video that we obtained earlier today from the army in Nepal, and it shows rescuers inside a tunnel of one of these hydroelectric plants. We got some clarity on these rescue operations late on Saturday night from an official in Nepal.
Let's bring up the statement for you. So, there was progress there. But at that tunnel, they have yet to establish communications with any of the trapped workers there. Now, families of the victims, they are stuck in this agonizing limbo, just waiting for answers. We also know that survivors, they are waiting for desperately needed supplies, like food, clean water and hygiene projects, products that have not been able to reach them, because the roads are impossible.
And meanwhile, rescue workers, they're trying to clear the roads, especially the roads between Nepal and Tibet. And they are addressing the challenge at hand. Watch this.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
WU XIN, COMMANDER, RESCUE TEAM, CHINA ANNENG (through translator): The biggest challenge today is that the roadbed is completely gone, and we're operating in a very confined space. So, we've been using excavators to dig out the silt and relay, while reinforcing the foundation with large rocks. So, far, we've repaired about 150 meters of roadway, excavated 12,000 cubic meters of silt, and repaired 100 meters of washed-out roadbed. We've reopened the first curve, where the roadbed had been entirely destroyed. That gives us a solid footing for the next phase of rescue and relief work.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
LU STOUT: Now, the finance minister of Nepal told Reuters that the country needs $4 to $5 billion U.S. dollars to rebuild. They need tunnel experts, they need technical training, and they also need the funds, the aid to be able to rebuild their communication lines, as well as their transport links, including all of those fallen, destroyed bridges and roads. Salma.
ABDELAZIZ: Kristie Lu Stout, as you're speaking there, we're looking at those images of the attempts to rebuild, reconnect these communities, and it's just gut-wrenching when you think about the so many people that are still missing and the families that are waiting for those answers. Thank you so much for joining us.
[04:10:00]
I want to go next to Kathmandu, where we are joined now by Suraj Sigdel. He is the Nepal country director for the organization, the humanitarian organization, Mercy Corps. Thank you so much, first of all, for joining us in the middle of what I know is a very complex and ongoing situation. This is an area, of course, that is familiar with flooding, that is familiar with natural disasters, but you've said this catastrophe is unprecedented. It's on an entirely different scale. Why is that? Explain that to me.
SURAJ SIGDEL, NEPAL COUNTRY DIRECTOR, MERCY CORPS: I mean, it was my first -- I think it was my first interview on the second day. So, what we saw and what people felt of the size of disaster, it was never happened in Nepal. Nepal is a mountainous country, a country with so many glaciers, and we do have this flash flooding regularly, and Nepal is gradually preparing with the early warning systems and making the people aware about evacuation, early identification, and all that. However, this one was, you know, like, even we were talking in our team, it even surprised the early warning system, you know, like early warning system was hit in such a way that they could not even warn others. So, even our, the mechanical technical part was also not successful to, you know, like relay or channel the message to the people. So, that's how big it was.
And, you know, like, we generally, when we talk about, when we listen about tsunami in different coastal areas, we used to think that Nepal is a landlocked country. So, we might not have the tsunami, but looking at this catastrophe, it was, I mean, like, equal to, if not more than any tsunami. So, what we have lost is not just, it's like lives we have lost. We have lost the infrastructure.
And Nepal, you know, like, we are developing a nation, and we have, you know, like, built all this infrastructure in a very gradual, slow motion. And now, you can see everything is gone. So, the road connection is gone. Communication is gone. The power is gone. So, that's why I call it unprecedented.
ABDELAZIZ: And as you're describing how the roads are gone, communications are gone, it seems right now that the main means of getting people out is, of course, airlifting them via helicopters. Can you tell me what is the priority list? Who gets evacuated first? And where are they taken once they are evacuated for treatment and for help? And is that process now working several days into this tragedy?
SIGDEL: It's -- I mean, like, they have the priority. So, if someone is evacuated and having the health issues or, like, other priorities, they are brought to Kathmandu, and they are directly taken to the hospitals for health, you know, like, checking and all that. And those who only need evacuation from the danger zone, they are, like, brought to their district head office or in the self-area. So, that is how the evacuation has been done.
Is it enough? No, it is not. Because, you know, like, we have limited helicopters. I think 20, 20-plus helicopters are being used right now. And that's all our capacity is. So, there are still many inaccessible areas, more in the Rasuwa district, who are still waiting for them to be rescued and identified. So, yes. I mean, government needs to boost up the rescue mission even further.
ABDELAZIZ: Only 20 to 20-plus helicopters, you estimate, that are ferrying people out, the most vulnerable, the ill, the wounded, many who are still there and need to be rescued. Talk to me about how things are getting into the disaster zone for those who are trapped there. How are you getting aid and supplies and whatever people need there on the ground? Because we understand, of course, food, water, all of that right now is in dire need on the ground. Do you have enough? Is it getting there quickly enough?
SIGDEL: Right. So, thanks to all the donors who have supported so far. At least people are joining hand. So, they're like national, international communities coming together to provide supplies, water, food items, emergency center and hygiene supplies. They are coming. So, government has planned to do the one door system. So, there are like collection centers in Kathmandu and in different part of the impacted areas. So, where people and the communities and the NGOs are depositing it, and it's being distributed through the government mechanism. That has been the case.
[04:15:00]
Again, is it enough? No, it is not. At least not to the Rasuwa district where people are scattered in different areas, and they are not accessed by any means. So, they are still being left out. However, the gradual supplies are being taken to Nuwakot, which is fairly a bit better, but to the Rasuwa which is more hard hitted, it needs further, you know, like supplies.
But the problem is, again, the transport, the road is gone. And the, you know, like the alternate road that was linked to Nuwakot which is quite narrow. So, we cannot take the big trucks. So, we need to go with the small minivans. So, which has a limited capacity. So, that is why also it is taking some time to reach out to the people.
And then, you know, like today itself, our one of the main highway, which is being closed again. It was closed for three days. It was open from yesterday. And today, because of the, you know, like hard hit by that water and mud, it is gone now. So, we are struggling with the access part.
ABDELAZIZ: Suraj Sigdel, they're talking about the struggle to access those who need this humanitarian help. And they need it now. By your estimate, it's only 20 plus helicopters are around that number that are ferrying people in, bringing help on the ground. It is simply not enough, though. Thank you so much for explaining the logistics behind that to us.
Now, very far away from the tragedy in the Himalayas, Nepali communities around the world are praying for the dead and missing. In Sydney, Australia, more than 100 people gathered for a candlelight vigil. One man whose friend is missing says the losses have really hit home.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
NAGENDRA ADHIKARI, HUMANITARIAN WORKER: We are sad for his family. We're still hoping for the good news. Not only that, some of my cousins, some of my relatives, their beloved are also missing. It's a huge disaster, and we are facing it.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABDELAZIZ: And here in London, a worship ceremony for a Hindu festival took a somber tone because of the devastating floods in Nepal. But it also doubled as a fundraiser, since many attendees have ties to that region. Organizers say they raised more than $8,000 for relief efforts and hope that they can collect more.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP) NARENDRA KANDEL, CHAIR OF COMMITTEE AT SHREE PASHUPATINATH TEMPLE: This is the temple, you know, where we always come together in difficult times. And people talk, you know, they try to support each other. And I mean, emotionally and at the same time, financially as well, you know, people have been very generous to support Nepal.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ABDELAZIZ: And in New York, Nepali communities gathered as they waited for news of relatives, friends and neighbors gone missing in those floods. They say the vigil was a way to share their sorrow together. For more information on how you can help the victims of this disaster, please visit cnn.com/impact.
I want to go back to our breaking news now. We first reported it last hour. Police in Switzerland say one person is dead and five others are injured. This is in a shooting at a dance party early Sunday. A manhunt is still underway in Aarau. This is a scenic town in northern Switzerland. Police say that the incident unfolded at a horse racing track where a rave was being held. Authorities added that the shooter's motive and the exact circumstances remain unclear.
Now, Switzerland has one of the highest rates of gun ownership in Europe, but violent shootings like this are rare and gun control laws in the country are extremely tight. We're going to bring you more information as we get it here at CNN.
And there's much more to come, including an attack near Kyiv that killed tens of people, but much of the damage appears to actually be not from those Russian drones or missiles.

ABDELAZIZ: Ukraine's president says a criminal investigation is underway. This is after one of the deadliest attacks in the Kyiv region since the start of the war. Russian drones hit an ammunition warehouse. This was on Saturday, and it likely set off the ordinances that were stored inside. It led to a series of explosions that lasted for hours and killed at least 38 people and wounded dozens more. Many of the nearby buildings were damaged, and now President Volodymyr Zelenskyy wants to know who is responsible for storing that ammunition so close to civilian homes.
For more on all of this, we're joined by Barbie Nadeau. She comes to us live from Rome. So, Barbie, President Zelenskyy is saying, look, someone is going to have to answer for this. What are you learning about the investigation so far?
BARBIE LATZA NADEAU, CNN REPORTER: Yes. You know, I mean, this is an incredibly disastrous situation, but it's the second time in recent months that the damage has been caused by the destruction of an ammunitions warehouse. And so, President Zelenskyy is looking into opening a criminal investigation, not into Russia, obviously, for launching this attack, but into his own military, and whoever made the decision to store this ammunition so close to a residential area.
And they're still -- more than 24 hours later, still trying to get through all of that rubble. And we've been hearing just horrible accounts from residents in that area about what they went through. Let's listen to this woman and what she went through that Saturday morning.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
HALYNA HORODETSKA, 70-YEAR-OLD LOCAL RESIDENT (through translator): A wall of fire was coming toward us. My son dropped to his knees and shouted, Mom, get down. So, I dropped to the ground and started crawling home on my knees. It was impossible to walk because the wall of fire would have engulfed us, so we crawled on our knees.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
[04:25:00]
NADEAU: You know, that's just a horrifying situation for that woman and for her son and for all of those residents, of course, and people who've lost loved ones. This comes in the midst of a very violent, ongoing attack for these jet-powered drones and missiles over the Ukrainian capital and the surrounding areas. For the last couple of days, we've got reports of multiple air raid sirens basically just stopping the city and those suburbs, people on their tracks, going down into bomb shelters, closing schools and shopping malls and banks and businesses.
And, you know, we're talking about four years into this war, looking at another long, hard winter ahead for those people, and now it seems that these, you know, ammunition warehouses are really causing a threat for people who are living in these areas, Salma.
ABDELAZIZ: Barbie Nadeau there, reporting from Rome, in such important context that this has happened recently. Thank you.
An Israeli airstrike has reportedly killed three people in the occupied West Bank. The IDF says it was targeting a Hamas militant who was joined by two accomplices. This is in the northern city of Jenin, and it happened on Friday. Now, Israel claims the militant had international ties with Hamas members and was linked to attacks on Israeli citizens and security forces.
Hamas condemned the attack but did not claim the alleged operative who was targeted. Palestine Red Crescent medics say they were briefly detained when Israeli ground troops later raided the strike zone, and they were prevented from transporting victims to the hospital. The IDF has yet to respond to those statements.
Now, to Venezuela, where the interim president there says a new oil deal with the U.S. is set to last for 25 years. The agreement gives the U.S. control over about a fifth of Venezuelan reserves, that's 65 billion barrels. Washington says the move is going to double America's oil reserves and lower gas prices. Caracas says the deal will help revitalize Venezuela's economy by transforming energy reserves into resources for social and housing programs.
Still to come here on CNN Newsroom, after a failing glacier triggered devastating floods in Nepal and China, families around the world are waiting for news of their missing loved ones.
And an update on a father and son who were detained by ICE in Texas, what their family has to share about the situation.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
[04:30:00]
ABDELAZIZ: Welcome back to CNN Newsroom. I'm Salma Abdelaziz in London.
The death toll from those devastating flooding along China's-Nepal's border has now risen to 750 people killed, and more than 3,000 people are still missing. The vast majority of them are in Nepal, and rescue workers are right now searching for any remaining survivors. Residents whose homes were buried in mud or were washed away are waiting for aid. And Nepal's foreign minister told the Reuters news agency that the country needs foreign specialist help for tunnel rescues, DNA testing, and restoring transportation and communication after the disaster. Hundreds of those missing in the disaster area are foreigners.
Some come from the U.S., and families are, of course, anxious to hear from their loved ones or simply find out what happened to them. CNN's Rafael Romo brings us some of their stories.
(BEGIN VIDEOTAPE)
RAFAEL ROMO, CNN CORRESPONDENT: According to the U.S. State Department, there are about 90 Americans missing after the devastating flood that hit Nepal's border with China on Wednesday. One of those missing Americans is Anil Goel from Virginia. According to his family, Goel, an avid hiker, had planned a multi-day trip to hike Mount Kailash near Nepal's border with China as part of a tour group.
But his son, Anurag Goel, told CNN this morning that the family hasn't heard from his father or the group since the day before the disaster. Anurag Goel says the last communication from the group happened less than an hour before the flooding happened in that area around the border.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
ANURAG GOEL, FATHER ANIL DURAG MISSING IN NEPAL WITH TOUR GROUP: They were known to last be at the Nepal side of the Nepal and Tibet border. That's called Gyirong Crossing. And they were last known to have received their departure stamp on their passports around 8:35 a.m. to depart Nepal, enter Tibet. And there were also some messages through WhatsApp exchanged around that time. So, that was roughly 45 minutes before the flooding took place in that area.
(END VIDEO CLIP) ROMO: Goel also told CNN that, quote, "There's been a large lag in communication from the affected region that has made it even more difficult for families like his who are desperate for any news about their loved ones."
Last night in Hicksville, New York, people came together for a solemn vigil. Leaders from the Long Island Nepalese Society organized the vigil to gather in solidarity, prayer and hope, as CNN affiliate, News 12 Long Island, reported. A man who worked as a teacher in Nepal and now lives in the United States told News 12 he lost several people he knew, including former students and neighbors.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
DIAWAKAR PANDE, NEPALI COMMUNITY LEADER: I was the teacher in there. I lost my three students in there. One, my neighbor people, I lost in there. A lot of the people I lost in this time, in this incident.
MYAM JIRI, LONG ISLAND NEPALESE SOCIETY: It's very heartbreaking to all of us to see our fellow country people losing their lives and their houses in a matter of a few minutes.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
ROMO: A State Department spokesperson told CNN the United States government is sending aid to Nepal, including food, and that the first trucks carrying assistance are on the way. The United Kingdom has pledged nearly $6.8 million in assistance, and the European Union said it would provide $2.3 million in aid.
Rafael Romo, CNN Atlanta.
(END VIDEOTAPE)
ABDELAZIZ: OK. We have breaking news coming in to CNN just now. Voters in Iceland have rejected a nationwide referendum to resume talks to join the European Union.
